Lütken. Part I. 1855. p. 290.} it will fall out as of itself that such a whole
must go together with its parts, and these therefore answer to one another. But
if it is now, in accordance with the law of development holding for

% --- p. 268 ---
organisms, natural that the parts and the whole answer to each other in the
lower and simpler forms, then one must surely find it just as natural that the
same is also the case in the higher and more composite ones; if nature can bring
forth in ``the fringe-mouth's'' skeleton a notochord
(\textit{chorda dorsalis}) without any final causes or preceding speculations,
why should she not also be able, without express final cause, to bring forth the
vertebral column in a human skeleton? In consideration of this we then grant
Kant that we may employ teleology only as a regulative principle, in so far as
our discursive understanding is not quite capable of comprehending the organic
products of nature.''

Once the physical view has in this way first been sharply stated and set over
against the equally sharply stated teleological one, it must be possible to show
precisely where the one-sidedness lies and wherein the error consists. We may in
this connection at once bring forward the following: \emph{the organic concept
of wholeness is superficially grasped by the physicist; the assertion that the
organic purpose should be impossible without a human self-consciousness is
untenable; so far is it from being the case that metaphysics' final cause}
(\textit{causa finalis}) \emph{should be only a powerless thought as against
physics' efficient causes} (\textit{causæ efficientes}), \emph{that it is on the
contrary the cause of reason which alone yields the originally true, in itself
sufficient causality.}

That the concept of the organism must coincide with the carried-through concept
of wholeness is evident. But when the concept of wholeness is to be carried
through, one cannot, as has been shown earlier, remain standing at the
mechanical whole. What Kant ascribes to our discursive understanding does not
concern the understanding but only the understanding of the mechanical --- a
mechanism of understanding answering to

% --- p. 269 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
the mechanism of nature. That the human understanding is very well capable of
thinking, and so far surely also of comprehending, ``products of nature in which
the whole is the parts' ground of coming-to-be and their prius,'' Kant has not
merely conceded but even himself clearly proved\footnote{Zu einem Dinge als
Naturzwecke wird nun \emph{erstlich} erfordert, daß die Theile (ihrem Dasein und
der Form nach) nur durch ihre Beziehung auf das Ganze möglich sind. Denn das
Ding selbst ist ein Zweck, folglich unter einem Begriffe oder einer Idee befaßt,
die Alles, was in ihm enthalten sein soll, a priori bestimmen muß. Sofern aber
ein Ding nur auf diese Art als möglich gedacht wird, ist es bloß ein Kunstwerk,
d. i. das Product einer von der Materie (den Theilen) desselben unterschiedenen
vernünftigen Ursache, deren Causalität (in Herbeischaffung und Verbindung der
Theile) durch ihre Idee von einem dadurch möglichen Ganzen, (mithin nicht durch
die Natur außer ihm) bestimmt wird. Soll aber ein Ding, als Naturproduct, in
sich selbst und seiner inneren Möglichkeit doch eine Beziehung auf Zwecke
enthalten, d. i. nur als Naturzweck und ohne die Causalität der Begriffe von
vernünftigen Wesen außer ihm möglich sein; so wird \emph{zweitens} dazu
erfordert: daß die Theile desselben sich dadurch zur Einheit eines Ganzen
verbinden, daß sie von einander wechselseitig Ursache und Wirkung ihrer Form
sind. Denn auf solche Weise ist es allein möglich, daß umgekehrt (wechselseitig)
die Idee des Ganzen wiederum die Form und Verbindung aller Theile bestimme....
[For a thing as a natural end there is required, first, that the parts
(as to their existence and their form) are possible only through their relation
to the whole. For the thing itself is an end, hence comprised under a concept or
an idea which must determine a priori everything that is to be contained in it.
But in so far as a thing is thought as possible only in this way, it is merely a
work of art, that is, the product of a rational cause distinct from the matter
(the parts) of the thing, whose causality (in the procuring and combining of the
parts) is determined by its idea of a whole thereby made possible (and hence not
by the nature outside it). But if a thing, as a product of nature, is
nevertheless to contain in itself and in its inner possibility a relation to
ends --- that is, to be possible only as a natural end and without the causality
of the concepts of rational beings outside it --- then there is required,
second, that its parts combine into the unity of a whole by being
reciprocally cause and effect of each other's form. For only in such a way is it
possible that conversely (reciprocally) the idea of the whole should in turn
determine the form and combination of all the parts....] Kritik der
Urtheilskraft, Gesammtausg. Vol. VII. Leipzig, 1839. p. 245.}. Strange, on the
other hand --- indeed, if one did not know the critique of reason
(cf.\ Phil. Propæd. pp. 23--31) that furnishes the foundation of the whole
Kantian philosophy, altogether incomprehensible --- is it that Kant can regard
it as an imperfection in the human understanding that it sees the organic
products of nature under ``the point of view of the concept of purpose,'' and can
hold that ``an intuitive understanding'' would be able to comprehend the organism
without the concept of purpose. What, then, is an organism? Kant answers: ``ein
organisches Produkt der
% (as printed in the Danish, the page break falls inside this German
%  quotation, between „der“ and „Natur“; the marker is set at the same point.)
% --- p. 270 ---
Natur ist das, in welchem Alles Zweck und wechselseitig auch Mittel ist.'' [an
organic product of nature is that in which everything is end and reciprocally
also means] The organized product is thus not accidentally, but according to its
concept --- indeed according to the definition itself --- a teleological product.
``Nichts in ihm ist umsonst, zwecklos, oder einem blinden Naturmechanismus
zuzuschreiben'' [nothing in it is in vain, purposeless, or to be ascribed to a
blind mechanism of nature] (Kr. d. Urthlskr. p. 248). But when the organic is
precisely a teleological, then it follows from this that an understanding for
which the teleological has no validity cannot ascribe validity to the organic
either. The intuitive understanding which would not need the concept of purpose
would then have to be an understanding for which the organic existed as little
as the mechanical. The nature which such an understanding --- denying both the
organic and the mechanical --- were to comprehend out of one principle would then
not at all be the nature that is in question in natural science and in
philosophy.

If the categories \emph{purpose and intention} could not be categories of
nature, because nature lacks self-consciousness and arbitrariness, then the
categories \emph{disposition and preformation} could not hold in nature either.
But what would become of the ground of nature (\textit{natura naturans}) and its
productions if categories like disposition and preformation were to fall away?
Is there not in every original disposition of nature a subjectivity without
consciousness? Do not the ideal determinations of the preformations precisely
yield a system of thoughts without a thinking I, categorical demands of will
without arbitrariness? Nature, one says, cannot intend anything, since she can
neither see nor hear, neither think nor will; but when the talk is of what is
essential and original in all seeing and hearing, all thinking and willing, it
will surely be readily evident \emph{that here there is a pervasive difference
between nature herself and her individuals.} Of the individual it holds that it
cannot possibly see without eyes, think without a brain, will without finite
knowing; for the individual the alternatives hold: either a seer, and then

% --- p. 271 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
with eyes, or without eyes, and then a blind man; either a thinker, and then with
a brain, or without a brain, and then a thoughtless one; either a will, and then
with self-feeling and knowing, or without knowing, and then without will. That
such alternatives, on the other hand, do not let themselves be applied to nature
herself is incontestable.

``The doctrine of the brain,'' says Lewes, ``is taken in two different ways by two
opposed schools. The one regards the brain as the \emph{original source}, the
other regards it as the \emph{instrument} that steps in between. According to the
one, the brain thinks as the stomach digests. According to the other, the brain
is the instrument of thinking, an instrument on which the soul plays as a
musician plays on a piano. However opposed these schools are, they meet on
neutral ground when they come to physiology. It is a matter of indifference which
side we take: the observed phenomena are the same, whether one regards the brain
as an organ or as an instrument. An out-of-tune piano gives inharmonious music,
be the musician never so able. A fourpenny whistle can never have the ring of a
trumpet. It is thus evident that the character of the sound produced must be
dependent on the instrument's construction and condition. This conclusion is the
only one that concerns the physiologist''\footnote{Hverdagslivets Physiologie.
Part II. p. 6.} --- But the consequence of such a way of seeing is surely
striking!

Even if we will for a moment settle down with the materialistic assertion
(cf.\ Propæd., pp. 177--78) that the organs are formed, not \emph{in order that},
but \emph{so that} they can be used in a determinate way, even the very strictest
materialism cannot deny that between the organ and the function --- between the
eye and sight, the ear and hearing, the brain and thinking --- there is not an
accidental but an essen%
% --- p. 272 ---
tial and naturally necessary connection: ``Es ist so unmöglich, daß ein
unversehrtes Gehirn nicht denkt, wie es unmöglich ist, daß der Gedanke einem
andern Stoff, als dem Gehirn als seinem Träger angehöre'' [it is as impossible
that an uninjured brain should not think as it is impossible that thought should
belong to any other material than the brain as its bearer] (Moleschott). With
what necessity the conscious thoughts proceed from the brain's organic activity,
C. Vogt has stated most pithily in the following declaration: ``Die Gedanken
stehen in demselben Verhältniß zu dem Gehirn, wie die Galle zur Leber oder der
Urin zu den Nieren, um mich einigermaßen grob hier auszudrücken.'' [thoughts
stand in the same relation to the brain as the bile to the liver or the urine to
the kidneys, to put it here somewhat coarsely] Here, then, when we take it
``einigermaßen grob,'' there is a quite natural agreement between the unconscious
and the conscious functions --- indeed one so original that the latter are only
as a reflected splendour, an involuntary reflection of the former. The
unconscious activity which forms the brain must then, on this conception, be at
least as thinking and as rich in thought as the consciousness that is formed by
the brain's thoughts; the unconscious activity which, in forming an eye, solves a
system of optical problems must then surely be at least as sure of visual
impressions and corresponding images as any seeing individual. But visual
impressions and corresponding images are only for an essential seeing; there is
then a seeing without an eye --- the seeing, namely, that organizes an eye; there
is a thinking without a brain --- the thinking, namely, that organizes a brain.

Now if the functions by which the organs of consciousness are formed are
essentially identical with the functions of consciousness that follow from them,
then these functions are thereby at the same time determined as the purpose for
which the organs are means. And what physiologist would declare that eye well
organized which lacked a requisite necessary for the activity of sight? But if
the requisites of sight furnish the standard by which the eye's perfection must
be judged, then sight is purpose and the eye means. Were the well organized

% --- p. 273 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
brain only the realization of a real possibility, not the completion of a
disposition determined in itself and expressly aiming at conscious thinking, then
a human brain that was altogether unfit for thinking, but otherwise a healthy
organ in a healthy organism, would also have to be regarded as well organized.
But if a brain unfit for thinking must be regarded as abnormal, if the physical
conditions of thinking are to furnish the standard by which a well organized
brain must be judged, then thinking is purpose and the brain means.

As the blind seer, the thoughtless thinker, the will-less willer, nature is
doubtless enigmatic, still more enigmatic than Egypt's Sphinx; but this enigmatic
character disappears as it becomes clear to us that the contradictions adduced
concern only our superficial conception, not nature. Nature, giving birth and
bringing forth, has in objective reflection, in original concrete unity, all the
categories that later arise in consciousness --- categories which are then in
subjective reflection separated as belonging partly to sensation, partly to
thinking, partly to those drives and promptings of nature against which
arbitrariness breaks\footnote{``Everywhere in the animal kingdom we see examples
of how the organs' quiet and calm activity, unconscious for the animal, passes
over into and develops into voluntary actions which the animal undertakes
according to an inner dark and irresistible intimation (for example the
art-drive)''... J. Steenstrup. Om Forpl. og Udvikl. gj. vexl. Generationsr.
p. 66.}; with all its immediacy nature has essential inwardness. There does
indeed run a contradiction through the kingdom of nature --- the fundamental
contradiction, namely, that nature's content on the one side is taken up
completely into the totality of existing things, as the ground into the
grounded, and yet on the other side reflects itself in a depth of inexhaustible
ideality. But this contradiction can never establish itself and so far can never
attain existence, since nature is incessantly in the act of

% --- p. 274 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{1}
% (COUNTER SET TO 1, NOT 0, ON PURPOSE, following the Danish. The page
%  carries only ONE footnote, but the printing marks it „**)“ at the foot of
%  the page while the anchor in the text is „*)“ — the two marks DISAGREE.
%  The printed note-mark „**)“ is reproduced; the discrepancy is a printer's
%  defect and is logged, not corrected.)
resolving it. It is just the resolution of this contradiction that entails that
nature must be in unceasing becoming, giving birth, bringing forth, transition
from lower to higher stages --- everywhere teleologically moved, for the
resolution is teleological\footnote{... ``von der Idee entfremdet, ist die Natur
nur der Leichnam des Verstandes. Die Natur ist aber nur an sich die Idee, daher
sie Schelling eine versteinerte, Andere sogar die gefrorne Intelligenz
nannten... Ist die Natur die Idee in der Form des Andersseyns, so ist, nach dem
Begriffe der Idee, die Idee darin nicht, wie sie an und für sich ist, obgleich
nichtsdestoweniger die Natur eine der Weisen der Idee ist, sich zu manifestiren,
und darin vorkommen muß.'' [estranged from the idea, nature is only the corpse
of the understanding. But nature is the idea only in itself, whence Schelling
called her a petrified, and others even the frozen, intelligence... If nature is
the idea in the form of otherness, then, according to the concept of the idea,
the idea is not in her as it is in and for itself, although nature is
nonetheless one of the ways in which the idea manifests itself, and must occur
therein.] Hegel. --- Cf.\ Phil. Propæd. §~36. pp. 190--98.}.

``But,'' one objects, ``even if it now be conceded that nature works
teleologically, the organic teleology still remains just as much incomprehensible
to us human beings. In the first place one cannot appeal to a final cause without
seeking the cause in an idea and thereby effacing the difference between product
of nature and product of art; and next --- and this is especially to be brought
forward --- one knows, as regards the activity of nature, no other causes than
the physical.''
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish: „ind=“ ends the line and the next line
%  begins „indrømmes“, doubling the syllable. Not reproducible in English and
%  so logged only.)

The first of these difficulties must disappear when one considers that a
production of an idea can just as well be unconscious as conscious, and that it
is, as has been shown above, precisely the opposition not between idea and
nature, but between a production conscious and unconscious in the
anthropomorphic sense, on which the difference between product of art and
product of nature essentially rests. Since the task, then, is to investigate what
a production of an idea means, and how far validity as metaphysical causality can
be ascribed to the idea over against the physical causes, the whole emphasis must
fall on the objection's second main point. We need in this connection only recall
what has already been developed.

% --- p. 275 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
That the physical cause has no particular independence of its own, apart from the
fundamental cause, has been shown from many sides in the foregoing. Mechanics
teaches us that every efficient cause is at bottom to be regarded as an integral
whose differentials are causal moments, and which can therefore again be
resolved into such moments\footnote{``If, to refer to an example, the material
volume $\varrho v = M$ shrinks to an extensive element $dM$, then the force (the
weight) $P$ shrinks at the same time to an intensive element $dP$.... If $p$
denotes the weight of a unit of volume, then the weight of a volume element,
expressed by polar coordinates, becomes $dP = pr^2 dr \sin a\, da\, do$. If this
equation is now integrated, a finite weight through a finite volume is
obtained.'' (Math. og Dial. p. 128). Just as ``every finite magnitude is, when
it is seen in the concept, a determination of the infinite, a definitive
expression for $\infty 0$'' (Math. og Dial. p. 115), so likewise is every
thing-cause, seen in the concept, only a definitive expression for an infinite
number of causal moments.}. Chemical analysis leads, when the problem of change
is to be solved, likewise to the result that the thing-cause, instead of being
something original, is on the contrary something derived, something grounded,
which again goes to the ground. Now if all thing-causes must be grounded, only to
go to the ground again, then it is not in the thing-cause but in the fundamental
cause that the true, self-valid causality must be sought; but the fundamental
cause is precisely a cause of reason --- indeed, with its peculiarly teleological
essential content, an idea-cause. If the laws of nature are, as physics expressly
presupposes, laws of reason, then reason's dominion is seen in the fulfilment of
the laws; but reason's dominion over things presupposes that things and
thing-causes are themselves in their essence rational.

For were there a cause whose validity did not rest on reason, whose existence
therefore fell outside the rational, then reason could exercise no power over it:
that reason

% --- p. 276 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
rules even over the irrational is to be understood only from this, that the
irrational --- reason's other, its impulse, barrier and resistance --- conditions
its energy, belongs to its essence, and is thus after all rational. It is thus
not reason which, in order to realize its demands, must find the physical causes
given and avail itself of them as the master-workman does of his tools; no, it is
conversely the physical causes which have their existence and significance in and
by the cause of reason. Now if it stands fast that the fulfilment of the laws of
nature would be impossible if reason were only an ideal ground (\textit{ratio})
without being a real cause (\textit{causarum causa}), if it had in the order of
things only a legislative and not at the same time an executive authority, then
it is now also easy to show what it means that the general cause of reason can
transform itself into an idea-cause peculiar in its form.

In so far as the thing-cause steps into the foreground, as in the elementary
totalities, otherness rules; in so far as the fundamental cause works in its own
peculiarity, selfhood rules. Under the pressure of otherness the teleological is
forced back; reason is seen in unalterable laws; but the laws' abstract necessity
stands in opposition to the things' abstract contingency: the fulfilment of law
grounded on contingent necessity (if \textit{a} is, then \textit{b} is) is still
imperfect. With selfhood the teleological is posited: in the ideal concretion of
means and purpose the abstract contingency disappears, for the contingent becomes
means; and thereby the abstract necessity is also cancelled, for the necessary
becomes end\footnote{There is a misunderstanding we must in this connection
particularly try to forestall. It is a prejudice which can still haunt clear
heads that natural teleology's significance should consist only in this, that we
mortals lay under infinite nature plans and aims fitted to our own subjective,
restricted mode of representation. --- ``Siehe'' --- says M. J. Schleiden ---
``wie ich mir die Sache denke. Stelle Dir eine Ephemere des Rheinufers vor; gib
ihr Bewußtsein und Verstand, natürlich ihrer Mückennatur gemäß; gib ihr und
ihrem Geschlechte die Fähigkeit der Tradition; --- am Mittag, in der vollen
Kraft ihrer Entwickelung, geräth sie an das Münster von Straßburg und mit edlem
und lobenswerthem Stolz stellt sie sich die Aufgabe, diesen Koloß kennen und
verstehen zu lernen; mit dem einzigen Maß, das ihr zu Gebote steht, mit ihrem
kleinen Mückenfüßchen beginnt sie rüstig die Arbeit, unermüdet, bis der Abend
des Tages und zugleich ihres Lebens sie unterbricht. Was sie begonnen, setzen
andere fort, und nach Tausenden von Generationen ist es ihnen endlich gelungen,
eins der Fenster in allen seinen Theilen auszumessen und zu beschreiben. Noch
ist so gut wie Nichts erreicht. Endlos liegt noch immer die Arbeit da für
Tausend nachwachsende Enkelgeschlechter. Aber die Eintagsfliege hat auch ihr
Selbstgefühl, ihren kühnen Forschergeist; sie fängt an zu philosophiren, wie
sie es nennt; mit ihrem Mückenverstande, der höchstens dem Begreifen eines
Spinnegewebes, „des schwächsten der Häuser“, wie der Koran sagt, und eines
Honigtropfens gegewachsen ist, versucht sie aus dem geringen Material ihrer
Kenntniß sich den genialen Gedanken Erwins von Steinbach zu entwickeln. Die
alberne Thörin! Nicht wahr?''
\par ``Aber so, ja noch tausendmal kleinlicher und unzulänglicher ist die
Stellung des Menschen zu Gottes großer Schöpfung. Was sollen wir sagen, wenn
nun der Mensch, „die kleine Narrenwelt“, die Frechheit hat, nach Zweckbegriffen
ein Unendliches beurtheilen zu wollen, von welchem er selbst kaum einen kleinen
Punkt kennt! Wem wollen wir denn den Zweck und das Bestreben, ihn zu erreichen,
unterlegen? Etwa der Natur? --- Was ist die Natur? --- Ein wesenloser
Schematismus, eine leere Form, unter welcher der unvollkommne und beschränkte
Mensch das All aufzufassen gezwungen ist. --- Oder etwa dem Schöpfer selbst,
dem heiligen Urheber aller Dinge? --- Ist es vom Wahnsinn grcß verschieden,
wenn ein Mensch sich einbildet, in dem Innern seiner zeitlichen
Erscheinungsform irgend einen Maßstab zu finden, mit dem er das Ewige, das
Zeitlose ausmessen könnte? --- Das aber ist die Teleologie.'' Die Beseelung der
Pflanzen. Studien. Leipzig, 1855. pp. 159--160.
\par [Schleiden's fable, in English: See how I conceive the matter. Picture to
yourself a mayfly of the Rhine bank; give it consciousness and understanding,
suited of course to its gnat-nature; give it and its race the capacity for
tradition. At midday, in the full strength of its development, it comes upon the
minster of Strasbourg, and with noble and praiseworthy pride sets itself the task
of coming to know and understand this colossus. With the only measure at its
disposal, its little gnat's foot, it vigorously begins the work, untiring, until
the evening of the day and at once of its life breaks it off. What it began,
others carry on, and after thousands of generations they have at last succeeded
in measuring and describing one of the windows in all its parts. As good as
nothing is yet achieved. Endlessly the work still lies there for a thousand
generations of grandchildren yet to come. But the mayfly too has its
self-regard, its bold spirit of inquiry; it begins to philosophize, as it calls
it; with its gnat's understanding, equal at most to comprehending a spider's web
--- the frailest of houses, as the Koran says --- and a drop of honey, it tries
out of the meagre material of its knowledge to develop for itself the inspired
thought of Erwin von Steinbach. The silly fool! Is it not so? --- But such, yes a
thousand times pettier and more inadequate, is man's position toward God's great
creation. What shall we say when man, the little world of fools, now has the
effrontery to want to judge by concepts of purpose an infinite of which he
himself scarcely knows one small point! To whom, then, shall we impute the
purpose and the striving to attain it? To nature, perhaps? What is nature? An
essenceless schematism, an empty form under which imperfect and limited man is
compelled to apprehend the All. Or perhaps to the Creator himself, the holy
author of all things? Is it far from madness if a man imagines he can find,
within his temporal form of appearance, some standard with which he might measure
the eternal, the timeless? But that is teleology.]
\par But such edifying expectorations, which might well become a thoughtless
catechism-parson quite as well as an inspired investigator of nature, have not
the least thing to do with natural teleology's objective concept and the problems
belonging under it. In his pious zeal to transform the rational into an infinite,
an ἄπειρον, which by denying the finite and overstepping measure and limit
becomes \emph{conceptless}, and which as something conceptless can then indeed be
comprehended neither by gods nor by men, the author quite forgets that infinite
reason precisely reveals its wisdom and proves its rationality by entering into
finitude and thereby into inner self-limitation. Since besides, moreover, men
like Plato and Aristotle, Leibniz and Wolf, Schelling and Hegel must absolutely
be among effrontery's representatives in ``the little world of fools'' --- ``wenn
nun der Mensch, „die kleine Narrenwelt“, die Frechheit hat, nach Zweckbegriffen
ein Unendliches beurtheilen zu wollen:'' --- one comes involuntarily to recall
the author's own telling words, though these telling words are admittedly placed
in another connection: ``Wie in aller Welt will man es denn rechtfertigen, daß
ein Mann deshalb, weil er ein guter Botaniker oder Physiker ist, auch nothwendig
ein guter Philosoph sein müsse?'' [How in all the world is one to justify that a
man, because he is a good botanist or physicist, must necessarily also be a good
philosopher?]}.
% (THE NOTE ANCHORED HERE RUNS OVER pp. 277 AND 278 in the Danish and is given
%  WHOLE at this anchor; pp. 277 and 278 therefore carry no mark and no
%  counter reset. The German is kept verbatim, its own „…“ marks included,
%  with a single running English gloss placed after it in its own \par.
%  PRINTER'S DEFECTS reproduced as printed, both in the p. 277 stretch:
%  „gegewachsen“ (a doubled syllable across the line break) and „grcß“ for
%  „groß“ (a Fraktur c for an o).)

% --- p. 277 ---
That the contingent becomes means is, in other words, to say that the particular
is individualized by being worked through by the
% (This printed page carries only two lines of running text in the Danish; the
%  rest of it is the continuation of the footnote anchored on p. 276.)

% --- p. 278 ---
general; that the necessary becomes end is seen from this, that by developing the
particular determinations of its disposition and its preformations it is likewise
individualized; \emph{organic individuation, the law's perfect fulfilment, is
thus the true natural expression for reason's content in reason's form, reason as
idea.} The teleological, in which the physical causes are moments of the
idea-cause, is thus not merely natural and rational but, as the original unity of
nature and reason, the only thing in truth rational: life, existing reason, is in
itself purpose, organic purpose, for life is idea.

Physics grasps the laws of nature in so abstract and one-sided a way that it
looks as though these laws not merely

% --- p. 279 ---
stood indifferent to the teleological but were even at strife with it. Thus one
treats in physiology of the laws for life's phenomena, but not for life itself.
And yet life has its own peculiar law just as surely as the lifeless has. If the
lifeless is essentially one with the selfless and thereby abandoned to otherness
and to indifferent changes, then life is essentially one with selfhood and
grounded in self-development. Now if the selfless has, in virtue of otherness,
its law, then life too must, in virtue of self-development, have its law: only in
the idea, whose teleology subordinates the lower law to the higher, can the
fulfilment of the laws of nature be fully cognized.

\emph{The law of otherness is matter's law, the fundamental law of the elements.}
The elementary changes have in them something dazzling, something that easily
leads the observer astray. Even one who can otherwise well understand that no
thing can either come forth from nothing or become nothing will yet, by keeping
to the sensuous, easily be caught in the false representation that motions and
manifestations of force can at a given time be called forth and then again
disappear as tracelessly as if they had never taken place. This fancy arises
from the fact that ``the transient activities'' are exchanged for others, lose
themselves in something else, and thus conceal their infinite continuation under
change's veil. Physics, which in thinking of all laws essentially thinks of the
law of otherness, has however at last itself discovered the error, in discovering
that no force is lost but that forces are exchanged, transformed, and replace one
another. It thus stands fast, in virtue of the law of otherness, that the
consequences of an activity once entered upon can never be annihilated --- that
what has happened can, in other words, never be made not to have happened. This
discovery is of far-reaching significance in the doctrine of life. In virtue of
the law of change every thing-cause is, as has already been shown in the analysis
of the chemical categories, a condition, and every efficient

% --- p. 280 ---
condition again a co-operating cause. The condition is indeed a moment, but a
moment brought together from without with another condition, another moment. Even
when the one moment with physical necessity entails the other, it will depend on
circumstances --- that is, on other conditions supervening from without ---
how far all the conditions contained in a given presupposition are to enter into
activity or not. In the world of conditions the law of otherness rules at every
point: to deify the law of otherness, to move in the infinite circuit of
conditions without discovering the unconditioned therein, is to think oneself
into the thoughtless, to live oneself into the lifeless.

\emph{The law of selfhood is life's law, the fundamental law of organisms.} Just
as it follows with inner necessity from the elementary's material nature that it
must be subject to the law of otherness, so it follows with inner necessity from
life's ideal nature that it must be subject to the law of selfhood. For what is
the ideal? The negatively real --- that which does not in finite fashion let
itself be either put together with another or separated from another, but
penetrates the other, is in essential difference from the other one with the
other, has its other in itself, and just for that reason relates itself through
the other to itself. But to relate oneself through the other to oneself, and
thereby to develop otherness within oneself, is to fulfil the law of selfhood.

For otherness and selfhood to be able to subsist in decisive opposition, the
opposition's factors must presuppose and confirm each other reciprocally:
\emph{it is otherness's determination to serve selfhood; it is essential to
selfhood to have need of otherness.} That the plant withers, that the animal
dies, shows clearly enough what it means that the living too is subject to the
law of otherness and therewith to perishability. The idea of life needs the outer
other in order through it to develop its disposition, its in%
% --- p. 281 ---
ward other. But the outer other is only a material condition; selfhood, which is
conditioned only by conditioning itself with inner necessity, is the unconditioned
in the system of conditions: the development of life is essentially
self-development, and self-development, teleologically regarded, development of
the idea. On the law of self-development rests the imperishability of the idea of
life.

Life itself --- the life that departs from the expiring plant --- the soul itself,
the soul that leaves the dying animal, cannot become nothing. Even were life's
activity in each and all subject to the law of otherness, it could yet, as we have
already seen, not possibly become nothing. Now if it already stands fast in virtue
of the law of otherness that the consequences of an activity once entered upon can
never be annihilated, then it stands just as fast in virtue of the law of selfhood
that the result of the self-development attained in the course of organization can
neither be balanced out by something else nor converted into something else. But
when the result of self-development can neither become nothing nor be converted
into something else, it must remain eternally in selfhood's remembrance.

The pure white light can be refracted in the prism and set itself out in coloured
rays; but when the prism is taken away and the refraction ceases, the colours
disappear and we have again, as before, the white light. It is otherwise with
life! The ideal refraction, the inner self-development which the principle attains
in the course of its organizing activity, is not disturbed because the material
organism is disturbed. What has happened cannot be made not to have happened; the
wealth of inner determinations that came forth in the course of the articulation
of the inward otherness dwelling in selfhood itself is an unforfeitable,
imperishable property which the principle must eternally retain in the idea. For
the light that is refracted in the prism both prism and refraction are something
accidental and indifferent; therefore the whole can as it were go back again ---
the refraction cease, the colours disappear, and the light after the refraction
become

% --- p. 282 ---
what it was before the refraction: the pure white light. But in this way the
principle of life --- for whose inner self-development the material organism is by
no means something accidental and indifferent, but on the contrary something
essential and necessary --- cannot possibly become again what it was before the
organization: the pure general principle.

To make the law of otherness life's fundamental law is nonsense; to let the law of
selfhood hold, and yet to assume that the development's ideal result should be
able to disappear and life to lose itself in nothing because the material
organism, subject to the law of otherness, falls forfeit to perishability, is a
senseless contradiction.

As the law of teleology and of life, the law of selfhood is then also
\emph{the law of remembrance.} So long as the highest determination of the
organic individuals is placed in this, that they are by propagation to secure the
species' maintenance, the purpose is only one-sidedly grasped.
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish: „knn“ as printed, for „kun“ --- a turned u.
%  Not reproducible in English and so logged only.)
The individuals developed to maturity live here for the coming-to-be of other
individuals, which are again to ripen so as to live for the coming-to-be of
others, and so forth. Instead of having its purpose in itself, selfhood has here
its purpose outside itself --- in another selfhood: it looks, from this point of
view, as though selfhood's highest organic act should precisely introduce the
dominion of otherness. This contradiction is resolved when the individuals'
highest determination is placed not in propagation but in remembrance. By
propagation selfhood enters upon otherness's material terms and then comes to
exist on those terms. Since every existence is perishable, the one must
successively step into the place of the other; but in so far as selfhood's
preservation is to consist solely and alone in propagation, this self-continuing
self-preservation is essentially a self-continuing self-dissolution. The organic
act of selfhood conquers perishability by propagating the perishable; the
continuous series of self-preservation shows a repe%
% --- p. 283 ---
tition of individuals whose restricted nature is incommensurable with
self-preservation's demand: the individual in the ten-thousandth link is just as
perishable as the individual in the tenth link; here in the infinitely continuing
series there is no essential progress, no final end.

Organic development is essentially self-development; but an existence whose
essence is self-development can be completed only in the completed
inwardizing of its developed content, i.e.\ in a corresponding remembrance of
existence. Only by remembrance is the perishable in existence cancelled; but only
by death does the carried-through remembrance become possible. So-called
``molecular death'' --- which is a condition for the reproduction, the
rejuvenation by assimilation, without which life's activity cannot be continued
--- shows us already an organic connection between death and remembrance. In
remembrance the immediate existence with its outer and material shape goes to the
ground; but the existence thus cancelled is not annihilated; it is on the contrary
completed --- in organic remembrance. Remembrance of existence is the final end of
the idea of life: \emph{only in substantial remembrance does the typical go
together with the teleological.} --- As model the paradigm can of course first be
actualized in a complete circuit of successive copies. If now, in the course of
life, one of these sensuous copies displaced the other without abiding result,
then the remembrance of the vanished images would take place only in our
consciousness, and the fundamental shape revealing itself through the many
alternating shapes would be a schema abstracted by our subjective thinking,
without objective reality. For the model to be actualized in truth, every one of
the vanishing copies must abidingly reflect its vanishing in the onward-striding
development; every reflex of a shape must be preserved in the organic remembrance
of selfhood as an objective moment, when the last stage --- the stage that marks
the model's

% --- p. 284 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
visible completion --- is reached\footnote{Zunächst ist hier darauf ein
entschiedenes Gewicht zu legen, daß man unter dem Begriffe der thierischen
Individuen nur materiell abgeschlossene morphologische Facta subsumiren darf, daß
man also wohl von verschiedenen Formen der Individuen sprechen, aber nicht
einzelne Formzustände eines Körpers als eben so viele Einzelindividuen unter
einem die ganze Formenreihe begreifenden Gesammtindividuum begreifen darf, wie es
neuerdings \emph{Reichert} that, wenn nicht der wesentlichste Gesichtspunkt bei
Beurtheilung thierischer Individualität, welchen der morphologisch bestimmte
\emph{Körper} des Thieres bietet, verrückt werden soll. [First of all a decided
weight is to be laid here on this, that under the concept of animal individuals
one may subsume only materially self-enclosed morphological facts; that one may
therefore well speak of different forms of individuals, but may not comprehend
single states of form of one body as so many separate individuals under a total
individual comprehending the whole series of forms, as Reichert has lately done,
if the most essential point of view in judging animal individuality --- the one
offered by the morphologically determinate body of the animal --- is not to be
displaced.] Victor Carus. System der thierischen Morphologie. Leipzig 1853.
pp. 253--54.}. But the last visible image of the completion is itself a copy, a
vanishing image of sense; \emph{thus only when the last material shape dissolves
and vanishes into the paradigmatic inwardness, the selfhood remembering the
content of its development, is the circuit closed, and. the goal attained.}
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish, reproduced: „sluttet, og.“ --- a full stop
%  after „og“, where the sentence runs on into „Maalet naaet.“ The stray point
%  falls after „and“ in the English at the same place in the sentence.)

As the absolute final end, the idea's perfect form, the remembrance of existence
should then be the category in which existence's contradictions --- those of life
and of death --- at last found their resolution. But is this really so? Instead of
reaching a turning point where all contradictions are resolved, we seem on the
contrary --- this is at least the provisional impression --- to strike upon a
nodal point in which all contradictions are gathered, and out of which they again,
when reflection is set in motion, swarm forth in unsurveyable quantity. ``If a
remembrance of existence is to have reality, then an existence of remembrance must
also have reality. But what is an existence of remembrance? Something existing
which is not there! Such an existing thing cannot, for example, be at rest, for
what rests has existence only by an immediate opposition to what is moved --- a
finite opposition which takes place only in the outward world. Just as little can
there be in such an exis%
% --- p. 285 ---
ting thing any motion; for motion expresses transition, but an actual transition
presupposes an immediate otherness which remembrance cannot have. The remembrance
of existence cannot consist in a pure inactivity; in pure inactivity there is no
reflecting, and where there is no reflecting there is no remembering either. But
neither can the remembrance of existence be thought to consist in activity;
activity presupposes that actual oppositions are posited and overcome; but actual
oppositions with corresponding tensions are found only in an actual, not in a
cancelled existence. The existence converted into remembrance, and thereby
cancelled, cannot be there in time, for time demands a finite succession of
alternating states; but a remembrance in which everything is concluded and
completed puts everything at a stand. Just as little can a remembrance of
existence take place outside time, in the timeless; remembrance must consist in a
continual calling back of what has vanished, an unceasing repetition of what has
been; but such a calling back, such a repetition, must necessarily have moments of
succession and cannot possibly take place in a timeless medium.'' --- In this way
one might go on with contradiction upon contradiction to infinity.

It is however, on some reflection, by no means difficult to discover whence all
these contradictions really take their rise; they proceed one and all from
superficial criticism, from a misunderstanding of the idea's dialectical essence.
By setting the different categories one-sidedly and critically up against each
other --- rest against motion, inactivity against activity and so on --- one can
of course easily dissolve the ideal concretion into sheer contradictions. In the
idea itself there is neither rest for itself nor motion for itself, but motion in
rest, rest in motion. It is the fundamental error of the critical philosophy that,
instead of seeking the contradictions of the concepts of the understanding
resolved in the concrete idea, it on the contrary holds the concepts fast in
finite opposition to the idea and lets those antinomies which have

% --- p. 286 ---
validity only outside the idea come forward as though they expressly concerned the
idea's essence. By forcing on the idea what directly conflicts with its essence, it
is an easy matter to awaken doubt about the idea's objective reality.

\subsection*{b) The Stages of Life.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{b) The Stages of Life}

\emph{``In so far as nature is idea, the development of the concept of nature is a
task for philosophy; but since the idea as nature is only what it is in its
productions, phenomena and laws, the development of the concept's content must
necessarily belong under empirical inquiry. It is precisely a peculiarity of the
concept of nature that it separates and connects philosophy and empirical inquiry
with equal force''.} (Propæd. p. 191).

As life's ground the idea is nature; as organic nature the idea is life. The
cognition of what life is essentially presupposes, from whatever side the task be
regarded, a corresponding cognition of what nature is; those who see in life an
insoluble riddle must then also admit that nature, the natural itself, is an
insoluble riddle.

\emph{Is it possible for human consciousness to penetrate into nature's interior,
or is it impossible?}

A humane scientific answer will, as regards this question, hardly come out either
as a decisive denial or as a decisive affirmation. ``What is it that makes my body
grow and thrive so long as I only satisfy my hunger, my need to draw breath and to
keep my body warm? that makes my heart and my pulse beat their even measure
without my being even able to hinder it? What makes me, on the other hand, master
of my limbs? Even the infant child, stares at its bare legs and
% (as printed in the Danish: a comma after „Barn“, verified at 600 dpi.)
% --- p. 287 ---
plainly delights that they move at its pleasure, and instantly as it pleases it
\dots\ More perhaps we all prize being able to see, hear and speak; everyone knows
something of the wonderful structure of the eye and the ear, and does not
unwillingly hear it explained somewhat more closely. The answering of all these
questions, the enlightenment about all these objects, belong under physiology.
You must not indeed believe that the answer or the enlightenment is always
decisive; into nature's real interior no human eye penetrates. But to see what is
given, and to divine what still lies hidden, also yields a satisfaction which no
one who has a sense for nature should deny himself.'' (Eschricht).

From culture's general standpoint such a moderate way of seeing is doubtless on
the whole to be recommended; but when the question of cognition transforms itself
into a scientific question of principle, the middle-way theory with all its
moderations is indeterminate, hovering, insufficient. What does it mean that the
human eye can indeed in a way penetrate into nature's interior, but yet not into
``nature's real interior''? What difference is there here, then, between nature's
real and unreal interior? It sounds from the one side:

\begin{quote}
\noindent „Ins Inn're der Natur dringt kein erschaffner Geist,\\
Zu glücklich, wem sie nur die äuss're Schale weis't!“
\end{quote}

\noindent from the other side:

\begin{center}
„Ins Inn're der Natur ---“\\
O Du Philister!\\
„Dringt kein erschaffner Geist.“\\
Mich und Geschwister\\
Mögt Ihr an solches Wort\\
Nur nicht erinnern;\\
Wir denken: Ort für Ort\\
Sind wir im Innern.“
\end{center}

[Haller: Into the interior of nature no created spirit penetrates; happy enough he
to whom she shows even the outer shell. --- Goethe, in reply: Into the interior of
nature --- O you philistine! --- no created spirit penetrates. Of such a saying
you had better not remind me and my kin; we think: place for place, we are in the
interior.]
% (QUOTE BALANCE, as printed in the Danish: the Goethe reply closes with a
%  „…Innern.“ closing mark for which no opening mark was set --- the printing
%  gives opening marks only to the two lines quoted back from Haller. Kept as
%  printed; this is the single unmatched closing mark in the region.)
% --- p. 288 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
There is in these expressions of feeling from both sides something that appeals to
sound sense and something that repels it.

To deny the human mind all capacity for cognizing the essence of things will not
do (Propæd. p. 63); if nature's kingdom is indeed a kingdom of mysteries, it is
still a kingdom of law-bound mysteries\footnote{These --- psychology's ---
mysteries will presumably never be cleared up; meanwhile the physiologists'
endeavours have made it possible at least to create a science of those phenomena
of life which stand in connection with the nervous system; and ``thus'', to use
Professor Huxley's fine expression, ``there has been added to science, out of that
lawless mysteriousness which is ignorance's preserve, one more great province ---
\emph{the realm of law-bound mysteriousness''}. (Lewes).}. To concede to the human
mind a capacity for an absolute knowing, a complete penetration into nature's
innermost, will not do either (Propæd. pp. 66--67); the speculative philosophy
which has spoken the boldest word about nature's transfiguration in spirit ---
``So ist die Natur die Braut, mit der der Geist sich vermählt'' [thus is nature the
bride with whom the spirit weds] (cf.\ Propæd. p. 193) --- has also, as regards the
facts and laws decisive for the cognition of nature, made itself guilty of the
greatest blunders. Even Goethe, who with his inspired view of nature --- ``wir
denken: Ort für Ort sind wir im Innern'' --- seems to place himself so
unconditionally on the standpoint of ``absolute knowing,'' yet changes key when in
his elegy ``Die Metamorphose der Pflanzen'' he profoundly reminds us of ``ein
geheimes Gesetz, ein heiliges Räthsel.'' [a secret law, a holy riddle]

If it be conceded that the human eye can penetrate into nature's interior only to
a certain degree, a certain limit, then the question is: \emph{to what degree, to
what limit?} The science which answers this question in principle is

% --- p. 289 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
not natural science but philosophy\footnote{Philosophy is, namely, a scientific
doctrine of the idea (Propæd. pp. 118--198) under the presupposition that it is at
the same time a scientific doctrine of cognition. (Propæd. pp. 5--67).}. ``We meet
here the remarkable thing, that the science which alone among all rightly
understands how to investigate nature's objects, nature's forces, nature's laws
--- in short, everything that is nature's --- nevertheless becomes embarrassed and
begins to stammer and lisp when it is to say determinately what nature is \dots\
That natural science cannot say what nature is must not, however, be
misunderstood, as though it either did not acknowledge the reality of the concept
of nature or were not in a position to connect a determinate thought with the
word: nature; on the contrary! what nature is, natural science can in its own way
present clearly enough --- but, be it noted, by presenting not nature itself but
nature's facts and laws, thus not directly but mediately, and that for the good
reason that nature is idea, and no science of experience is in a position to
present the idea as idea.'' (Phil. Propæd. p. 191). ``Every science presents a
content of the idea, a side of the idea, a particular idea, but mediately; only
philosophy sets out to present the idea as idea.'' (Propæd. p. 118).

Just as the cognition of the idea's logical essence rests on insight into the
system of the logical categories, so the cognition of the idea of nature rests on
clear and distinct insight into the system of the categories of nature. How
impossible it is for philosophy to secure any true conception of the concepts of
nature when it is not step by step guided by empirical inquiry has in the
foregoing been impressed from many sides and in many ways. In this connection it
shall now be just as emphatically impressed that all natural science's
discoveries, even were these brilliant discoveries continued through millennia,
are yet in all their manifoldness insufficient

% --- p. 290 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
to open man's eye for nature's ``real interior,'' if philosophy does not go along
and work up the system of the categories answering to the facts in such a way that
the idea mirrors itself therein\footnote{As sure as natural science is when facts
and laws are in question, just as unsure is it when it comes to judging the
objective validity of the categories (cf.\ Propæd. p. 182).
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish: „Kategorietnes“ as printed, for
%  „Kategoriernes“ --- a t standing where the r belongs. Not reproducible in
%  English and so logged only.)
It is not enough that we, on physiology's own admission, ``do not know what life
is''; we ``do not'', according to physics' explanation, ``really know either what
electricity is, what force is'', and so on. Were one in this respect to take
natural science's turns of phrase in dead earnest and draw the consequences, then
a real science of nature would, for all the discoveries, be an impossibility. It
looks so modest and yet so acute, so resigned and yet so highly scientific, when
one declares just like that: ``we do not know what force is.'' What strict demands
on human knowing are here intimated by such a declaration! ``Die Kraft ist nichts
als eine verstecktere Ausgeburt des unwiderstehlichen Hanges zur Personification,
der uns eingeprägt ist; gleichsam als ein rhetorischer Kunstgriff unsres Gehirns,
das zur tropischen Wendung greift, weil ihm zum reinen Ausdruck die Klarheit der
Vorstellung fehlt. Ju den Begriffen von Kraft und Materie sehen wir wiederkehren
denselben Dualismus, der sich in den Vorstellungen von Gott und Welt, von Seele
und Leib hervordrängt.'' [Force is nothing but a more concealed offspring of the
irresistible bent to personification that is imprinted on us --- as it were a
rhetorical device of our brain, which reaches for the tropical turn because it
lacks the clarity of representation for a pure expression. In the concepts of
force and matter we see recurring the same dualism that presses forward in the
representations of God and world, of soul and body.] (Du Bois-Reymond.
Untersuchungen über thierische Electricität. Vorrede). ---
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT reproduced, the German being verbatim: „Ju den Begriffen“
%  as printed, for „In den Begriffen“ --- a turned n.)
The deeper ground of so desperate a surrender of all human knowing is however not
difficult to discover. The matter is simply this, that the investigator of nature,
who is in the habit of referring all cognitions to
% --- p. 291 ---
% (the whole of this page's foot in the Danish is the unmarked run-over of the
%  p. 290 footnote, given above at its anchor. No mark and no counter reset.)
immediate impressions of sense, goes astray in the determination of what can be
sensed and represented and what by its nature is neither to be sensed nor
represented but to be thought. That force does not let itself be immediately
represented is just as certain as that force is not blue, and that one is not
able under the microscope to point out particular streaks of force visible for
either attraction or repulsion; force is as force --- in opposition to matter as
the sensuous in the phenomenon's immediacy --- a concept of reflection. Now if
thinking, dazzled and blinded by the incessant sensing, microscoping and
representing, becomes to such a degree a stranger to itself that it cannot
recognize its own essential object even in an elementary concept like force, how
much less will such a blinded thinking then be in a position to cognize categories
like \emph{nature, life, soul,} which are far more reflected. By rejecting the
categories of nature reflected in themselves, one has however broken the staff not
only over philosophy's ``ideas'' but even over natural science's ``laws''; for the
laws too are categories of reflection: one never gets past Hume!}.

What has here been said of the idea of nature in general holds in a particular
sense of the idea of life. How far philosophy is here in a position to transform
the actual facts into ideal moments will then best be seen when the content is
articulated, when the task is taken concretely --- when we, in other words,
attempt a presentation of the idea of life at its different stages as
\emph{plant life, animal life, human life.}

\subsection*{α) Plant Life.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{α) Plant Life}

As organic nature the idea has its first immediate existence in the merely
vegetating organism, in the plant. In so far as the vegetative organism is the
idea's imprint, the formative activity determined by the idea itself must in all
essential features necessarily be typical-teleological. But since telos is
immediately a vegetating living, and the cell already vegetates as living cell,
it is seen from this that in the plant world it is not so much the teleological
as rather the typical on which the manifold of characteristic forms essentially
rests. From selfhood's paradigm there proceeds, according to what we have seen in
the foregoing, the plant's peculiar shape; with the peculiar shape telos is
posited peculiarly; with the peculiar telos the vegetative means --- which,
essentially regarded, signify nothing other than the fundamental division of the
indwelling telos itself, motivated by finitude, relativity, mediacy --- have then
likewise become pecu%
% --- p. 292 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
liar. By virtue of the paradigms' characteristic manifoldness the general purpose
of plant life is thus realized in so manifold ways that the one fundamental idea
of vegetation is seen to unfold thereunder into a system of separate ideas. To
make clear what is of principle in this unfolding, we shall in the present
connection direct attention to the plant world's \emph{fundamental forms,
metamorphoses and individuations.}

\emph{αα) Fundamental forms.} One can, in agreement with ``the artificial'' as
well as with ``the natural'' system, divide plants into two large, comprehensive
groups: \emph{cryptogams} (the flowerless) and \emph{phanerogams} (flowering
plants); but the division's scientific worth will always depend on the peculiar
light in which the marks brought out are regarded.

That there shows itself here between the cryptogamic and phanerogamic formations,
in the whole as well as in particulars, a difference cannot be denied; but what
does such a difference really signify? The difference may confine itself to a
pure and bare unlikeness, whereby it then indeed loses all further interest,
unless it should serve a merely heuristic interest of classification\footnote{For
use in merely heuristic classification one seizes on any mark whatever, even the
most characteristic, in its pure contingency; the Linnaean system (the sexual
system), which is built on the number and remaining relations of the organs of
fertilization, furnishes a grand example of this. According to this system plants
which are otherwise altogether unlike each other can come to stand quite close
together when they happen to be alike with respect to the chosen mark; thus for
example a barberry (\textit{Berberis}) and a snowdrop (\textit{Galanthus}) both
belong to the 6th class, an ash (\textit{Fraxinus}) and a glasswort
(\textit{Salicornia}) both to the 2nd class, although nature by a highly different
mode of organization has separated the barberry widely from the snowdrop, the ash
widely from the glasswort. On the other hand, plants which in all else show
themselves mutually related can be removed far from one another when it happens
that they differ with respect to the chosen mark; thus, according to the Linnaean
system, agrimony (\textit{Agrimonia}) belongs to the 11th, meadowsweet
(\textit{Spiræa}) to the 12th, lady's mantle (\textit{Alchemilla}) to the 4th, and
salad burnet (\textit{Poterium}) to the 21st class, notwithstanding that by their
natural peculiarity they are all members of one and the same family, namely the
rose family. --- That stamens and pistils are moreover not only sure marks but
essential individual forms gets here no significance. What is artificial in an
``artificial system'' is that a natural mark is by abstraction torn out of its
natural connection and held fast for itself for use in classification.}. The
difference may go deeper, to the point of

% --- p. 293 ---
% (the foot of this page in the Danish carries the unmarked run-over of the
%  p. 292 footnote, spliced into the note above at its anchor. No mark, no
%  counter reset.)
heterogeneity, without its yet following from that that the heterogeneous relate
to each other as the less perfect to the more perfect, the lower to the higher.
But suppose even that the cryptogamic formation did relate to the phanerogamic
--- the spore-capsule to the seed-case, the spore to the germ, and so on --- as
the less perfect to the more perfect: from what point of view and by what standard
is the difference, transformed into a peculiar opposition, then to be more closely
determined?

Since plant life essentially goes up into organic forming, immediate organizing,
the reliable standard for lower and higher must indubitably be sought in the inner
organic constitution of what is organized. To assess this constitution the
relation between disposition and execution must stand clear before the
consideration. A disposition lower in itself may in a given case have found a rich
and many-sided execution, while the execution of a disposition higher in itself
has remained standing at a lower stage; to judge the opposition between the lower
and the higher it is thus not enough to look at the outward, the immediate shape;
here one must essentially look at the inward, \emph{at the fundamental form.}

We set the cryptogamic spore-case (\textit{sporangium}) against the phanerogamic
seed-case (\textit{pericarpium}). Were

% --- p. 294 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
we now to judge by the outward alone, then what is lower in its fundamental form
would doubtless very often present itself to us as though it were the higher.
Thus the mosses have a spore-capsule formed of a large complex of cells; the
capsule opens transversely, and in not a few cases in such a way that an elater
(\textit{elater}) between the lid and the capsule throws the lid off; the
so-called peristome has now a single, now a double crown of teeth, and so on:
here is a rich furnishing. In comparison with spore-capsules so richly furnished,
the seed-case in many a phanerogamic plant must undeniably look poor and appear to
us both simple and insignificant --- especially when this seed-case, as in
\textit{Lathyrus latifolius}, remains standing at the form of an ordinary flat
leaf. It is otherwise when we look at the fundamental form, the peculiar
disposition, and the morphological foundation answering to it.

By what, now, is the morphological foundation to be known? Naturally by the
element on which what is peculiar in the formation essentially leans. Such an
element is, as regards the spore-capsule's formation, \emph{the cell;} as regards
the seed-case, it is \emph{the leaf.} If we call the cell, so far as the organism
is concerned, the primary element, a formative element of the first order, then we
must call the leaf --- the formation in which the cell-element is reduced to a
moment --- a higher element, an element of the second order. However high the
formation of the spore-capsule may climb\footnote{In the lichens
(\textit{Lichenes}) the spores are, in a determinate number (4 to 8), surrounded
by a single elongated cell (\textit{theca}), which in this case is spore-case; a
usually large number of such spore-cases set together forms a spore-bed
(\textit{apothecium}). As regards the formation of such a spore-bed, the
organization is thus still so weak that it almost loses itself in aggregation.
That such an organic aggregation is however something more than a merely
mechanical one comes gradually to view in higher execution of the cryptogamic
disposition; but neither the mosses' nor the ferns' spore-capsules attain the leaf
form.}, its inner structure rests

% --- p. 295 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
% (in the Danish the foot of this page opens with the unmarked run-over of
%  the p. 294 footnote, printed ABOVE this page's own *) note; it is spliced
%  into that note at its anchor on p. 294. The counter reset serves this
%  page's own note.)
only on cells and aggregations of cells, whereas the lowest seed-case, as carpel,
is originally always a leaf. The lowest phanerogamic carpel thus stands, in virtue
of the fundamental form, higher than the highest cryptogamic
spore-capsule\footnote{To see what can be attained on a higher foundation in the
course of progressive formations and transformations, we need only cast a glance
at the more developed seed-cases. In the tamarind's fruit, which is yet still
formed of a single leaf, the seed-case is differentiated into inner and outer
epidermis; the intervening leaf-flesh (Diachym) is developed into a fruit-pulp
(\textit{pulpa}) whose cells swell with richly formed substances. Against such an
articulation the cryptogamic splendour is a vanishing thing. If we consider a
seed-case composed of several leaf-elements --- the orange's fruit, for example
--- then we find here a still finer, still richer articulation: an epidermis in
which a great number of glands containing ethereal oil come forward, a leathery
mass (Diploe, Diachym) which has provided a nourishing foundation for that array
of densely stacked, fleshy sacs containing the orange juice which proceed from the
pericarp's inner surface, and so on.
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish: „der har afgive:“ as printed, for
%  „afgivet“ --- a colon standing where the final t belongs. A letter-level
%  defect with no English counterpart; logged only.)
}.

For a closer determination of the opposition between the lower and the higher it
is further necessary to grasp the fundamental form \emph{teleologically.} We set
the cryptogam's spore-formation against the phanerogam's germ-formation. Were we
now to look away from the difference of the fundamental forms and take the purpose
abstractly, we should soon be done with the opposition. Spore-formation and
germ-formation are indeed both laid out for one and the same purpose:
propagation\footnote{What the opposition between the antheridia's sperm-filaments
(spermatozoa) and the central cell of the fruit-body (the archegonium) is in the
cryptogams, that is the opposition between the anther's male seminal material
(pollen, especially what is organized in the pollen tube, the elongated
pollen-grain) and the germ-cell enclosed in the egg's embryo sac in the
phanerogams.}. That

% --- p. 296 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
propagation, the purpose itself, is something essential goes without saying; but
--- one says perhaps --- ``the means of propagation, the way in which propagation
happens, seem to be something altogether unessential; or why should a propagation
by spore not be just as good as a propagation by germ?'' This way of seeing is
without idea. Yes, if the organic purpose really let itself be separated in this
way from the corresponding means, we should doubtless have to remain standing at
the tautology that propagation is propagation; but since ``the organic product of
nature'', to recall by these words of Kant what was developed earlier, is
precisely ``such a one in which each of its parts is aim and at the same time
means or instrument'', the general purpose becomes for each fundamental form
individualized in the means, the teleological becomes determined by the typical,
propagation becomes different from propagation\footnote{The difference between
propagation and propagation of which we here speak presupposes of course that
propagation's peculiarity is held fast, in so far as ``the law of propagation
expressed everywhere in living nature'' presupposes ``the distribution of sex upon
two and its opposition in two different beings.'' --- ``It is indeed generally
stated in every botany that hermaphroditism is the normal, i.e.\ commonest and
most natural, form for the reproductive relations of plants; but seen more closely
this is surely rather a turn of phrase inherited from earlier times, when the
doctrine of the so-called ``metamorphosis'' of plants had not yet exercised its
purifying and beneficial influence on the conception of the plant. The whole thing
here depends on what is to be regarded as the real individual in the plant, as the
real plant-unit.'' J. J. Sm. Steenstrup. Undersøgelser over Hermaphroditismens
Tilværelse i Naturen. Copenhagen 1845.}.

It is, be it noted, not a consideration of the phenomena in their immediacy, but a
cognition of the idea answering to the phenomena considered, of which the talk is.
To grasp the opposition between a higher and a lower as an

% --- p. 297 ---
opposition between what satisfies the idea and what does not satisfy it doubtless
betrays a serious misunderstanding; for the idea is satisfied by the lower just as
much as by the higher, precisely because in the immediate sense it is satisfied
neither by the one nor by the other, but first finds its essential satisfaction in
the dialectic of the opposition's terms. But when the dialectic of satisfaction
now shows itself in this, that the higher in its form satisfies precisely by its
characteristic opposition to the lower, and this lower conversely by its
characteristic opposition to the higher; when both, by being equal before the idea
--- in the same sense as when we say that the poor and the rich citizen are both
equal before the law --- have this essential equality reflected in an inequality
just as decisive for the idea --- as when we say that the inequality between rich
and poor is justified by the law that upholds the right of property ---; when it
is thus clearly seen that the lower's equality with the higher must in the idea
itself be grounded on the inequality: then it becomes a task, at least a
provisional one, to make clear to ourselves the demands contained in the idea of
propagation, as by a thought-experiment, in order thereafter to test how far the
given phenomena agree with what has been developed in such a thought-experiment.

May we now presuppose that the phanerogamic fundamental form belongs, within the
plant world, to a higher region than the cryptogamic, then we may also presuppose
that phanerogamic propagation must relate to cryptogamic as the higher to the
lower. If the idea of propagation posits an essential separation and yet an inward
union of the mother plant and its offspring, then --- this is a thought-experiment
--- the higher satisfaction of the fundamental demand will typically rest on this,
that the fertilization happens directly, that the embryo gets a nourishment
specially prepared for it by the mother body, and that the embryonal formation,
marked from the beginning by a deter%
% --- p. 298 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
minate mid-point, makes itself known in primitive articulations.

Now if this train of thought has reality, then it can hardly be doubtful that the
spore must relate to the germ as the lower to the higher. While the fertilization
of the phanerogam's egg happens directly, so that the fertilized germ-cell itself
yields the new centre, the express mid-point of the embryonal formation,
cryptogamic fertilization happens on the contrary indirectly, in so far, namely,
as it is not the spore\footnote{Die Sporen sind einer vorgängigen Befruchtung
nicht bedürftig; die Samen dagegen sind ohne eine solche nicht entwicklungsfähig;
und wenn sie auch äußerlich die gewöhnliche Gestalt und Ausbildung erreichen, so
fehlt ihnen doch der Embryo, auf dessen Gegenwart alle spätere Entwicklung beruht.
Man hat deshalb denselben Gegensatz dieser beiden Pflanzengruppen durch die
Unterscheidung von geschlechtslosen, Kryptogamen, und Geschlechtspflanzen,
Phanerogamen, bezeichnet, indem man den in der Thierwelt bekannten Begriff der
Sexualität auf die Pflanzen übertrug$\ldots$ Diese Anschauungsweise hat in neuerer
Zeit viele Ungunst erfahren$\ldots$. Es ist nahmentlich die eigenthümliche
Befruchtungstheorie Schleidens, welche der Parallelisirung thierischer und
pflanzlicher Verhältniße entgegentrat. Nach Schleiden ist es das Pollenkorn, der
bisher für das männliche Organ gehaltene Theil, aus welchem der Embryo des Samens
entsteht. Gleich einer Spore oder einer andern einzelnen Fortpflanzungszelle ist
der Pollenschlauch die entwicklungsfähige, und einer Befruchtung im Sinne der
thierischen Zeugung unbedürftige Grundlage der spätern neuen Pflanze. Aber seine
Lebensfähigkeit ist nicht von der Art, daß er unmittelbar der äußern Einflüßen
ausgesetzt, sich zu einem neuen Organismus, entwickeln könnte; er bedarf es
vielmehr, zuerst in eine bestimmt angeordnete organische Umgebung aufgenommen zu
werden, die ihm die zu den ersten Schritten seiner Bildung nothwendigen
Bedingungen gesetzlich und abgemeßen gewähre. So begiebt sich denn diese
keimfähige Grundlage der Pflanze in jene für weiblich gehaltene Organe des
Fruchtknotens, um hier, wie an eine Brutstelle, so lange gepflegt zu werden, bis
sie die nöthige Masse und Festigkeit innerer Verhältniße gewonnen hat, um in
unmittelbarer Wechselwirkung mit der Außenwelt sich weiter zu entfalten. Ich kann
aber das Bestreben, die sexualen Verhältniße der beiden Reiche zu parallelisiren,
nicht für eine grundlose abergläubische Richtung der Phantasie
ansehen$\ldots$. Es versteht sich übrigens von selbst, daß diese methodologisch
richtige Vermuthung analoger Einrichtungen bescheiden genug auftreten muß. [Spores
do not stand in need of a prior fertilization; seeds, on the other hand, are
without such not capable of development, and even if outwardly they attain the
usual shape and formation, they still lack the embryo on whose presence all later
development rests. One has therefore designated the same opposition of these two
groups of plants by the distinction of sexless plants, cryptogams, and sexual
plants, phanerogams, transferring to plants the concept of sexuality known in the
animal world... This way of seeing has in more recent times met with much
disfavour.... It is namely Schleiden's peculiar theory of fertilization that
opposed the paralleling of animal and vegetable relations. According to Schleiden
it is the pollen-grain --- the part hitherto held to be the male organ --- out of
which the embryo of the seed arises. Like a spore or any other single reproductive
cell, the pollen tube is the developable foundation of the later new plant, and
one not in need of a fertilization in the sense of animal generation. But its
capacity for life is not of such a kind that, immediately exposed to outer
influences, it could develop into a new organism; it needs rather first to be
taken up into a determinately ordered organic environment which may grant it,
lawfully and in measure, the conditions necessary for the first steps of its
formation. Thus this germinable foundation of the plant betakes itself into those
organs of the ovary held to be female, in order to be tended there, as at a
breeding place, until it has gained the necessary mass and firmness of inner
relations to unfold itself further in immediate reciprocal action with the outer
world. I cannot, however, regard the endeavour to parallel the sexual relations of
the two kingdoms as a groundless superstitious tendency of the imagination.... It
goes without saying, moreover, that this methodologically correct surmise of
analogous arrangements must come forward modestly enough.] Lotze. Allgem. Physiol.
pp. 558--61 and following.

The sexual opposition is --- for the plant organisms too --- of pervasive
significance. That the assumption of a ``Coelebogyne'' must rest on an illusion
already now seems to be pretty well settled. ``Da wir nun die Coelebogyne noch
viel zu wenig kennen, auch bisher für sie nur ein sehr beschränktes Material zur
Beobachtung vorhanden war, so ist die Frage der Parthenogenesis im Pflanzenreiche
noch keineswegs entschieden und sprechen die von Regel mitgetheilten Beobachtungen
nicht sehr zu ihrem Vortheil.'' [Since we still know the Coelebogyne far too
little, and since hitherto only a very limited material has been available for its
observation, the question of parthenogenesis in the plant kingdom is by no means
yet decided, and the observations communicated by Regel do not speak much in its
favour.] H. Schacht. Lehrbuch der Anatomie und Physiologie der Gewächse. Berlin
1856. Vol. II. p. 596. --- As regards parthenogenesis in the animal world, on the
other hand, cf.\ Th. E. Siebold. Wahre Parthenogenesis bei Schmetterlingen und
Bienen. Leipzig 1856.}, but the archegonium's
% --- p. 299 ---
% (the foot of this page in the Danish carries the whole run-over of the
%  p. 298 note, its Danish second paragraph included, given above at its
%  anchor; the counter is reset here for this page's OWN first note.)
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
central cell that is fertilized immediately\footnote{The fertilized central cell
then develops, in the fern especially, into a sporangium in which the germinable
spores come forth. ``The prothallium, which develops out of the spore, is a
heart-shaped, leaf-like body attached to the earth by rhizoids; on the
prothallium's underside are found many antheridia and a few fruit-bodies; the
latter are fertilized by sperm-filaments proceeding from the antheridia, and as a
consequence there grows out of the fruit-body a fern whose lowest part becomes the
rhizome and sends out adventitious roots.'' Chr. Vaupell. Planter. Naturhist.
p. 133.

The spore is expressly set against the seed because the typical point of view is
that the new system of individuations takes its beginning with the spore.}. In the
direct fertilization the germ's greater primitiveness over the spore is thus
already grounded. This difference now steps forward more clearly when the question
is of nutrition. For something non-independent --- a growth which is rather an
even continuation of something already given than anything peculiarly new --- no
particular housekeeping is of course introduced; if a housekeeping

% --- p. 300 ---
is to be introduced, a nourishment of its own prepared, then particular weight
must be laid on the independence of what is thereby to be nourished. Here we have
again the opposition between the cryptogamic and the phanerogamic. The central
cell of the cryptogamic fruit-body is --- in the higher forms --- referred to
sucking its nourishment from the whole remaining plant (in the ferns from the
prothallium), whereas the embryonal cell in the phanerogams begins by nourishing
itself from the mother cell's content (\textit{protoplasma}) and from provisional
formations within the mother cell; once the mother cell together with its content
is consumed, the nucleus' remaining mass (\textit{nucleus ovuli}) can also be
seized upon, as is usual especially in seeds without endosperm. The objection that
the cryptogamic mother organism steps into opposition to its offspring just as much
as the phanerogamic overlooks that the question here is not about the opposition
\textit{in abstracto} but about the opposition's typical expression. The objection
