that a nourishment ``sucked from the whole plant'' may for its use be just as
vigorous and sound as a nourishment specially prepared either in ``the mother
cell'' or in ``the nucleus' remaining mass'' for its use --- an objection thus
grounded on the tautology that a nourishment is always a nourishment, just as an
offspring is always an offspring --- must, in a connection where what is at stake
is precisely a typical expression of the determinations characteristic of a more
independent embryonal formation, be dismissed as without idea and unwarranted.

As regards the formation's point of departure, the primordial mid-point, and the
articulation's corresponding primitiveness, the phanerogam's advantage over the
cryptogam is evident. The spore --- which may for the rest be either a single cell
or a cell-aggregate --- betrays its weakly developed, indifferent condition in
this, that so far as observations go it never exhibits any polarity; whereas the
phanerogam's germ, even when it consists, as for example in Melampyrum, of only
two cells, expresses the articulation in

% --- p. 301 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
a polar opposition between an upward and a downward\footnote{The cell which has
been turned toward the micropyle (\textit{micropyle}) yields, namely, the root
end, while the cell turned away from the micropyle yields the upward-going part of
the plant.}. The cryptogam begins in the uniform; the phanerogam enters from the
beginning into a plurality of formal determinations. When the young cryptogamic
plant comes forth from the spore as prothallium (\textit{prothallium}), it is
neither anything else nor anything more than the cell it was before, only larger
and altered in outline; in most phanerogams, on the other hand, the germ's
articulation is so determinately expressed in axis and leaf --- indeed in leaves of
different orders: cotyledons, primordial leaves --- that one has even for that
reason called the germ itself the little plant (\textit{plantula}).

If cryptogamic propagation is in its essence so different from the phanerogamic,
then the organisms which are of so different a descent must also, when they are
considered in their complete development, display this fundamental difference in
all their main features. Set for example lichens against grasses, ferns against
acacias, and the typical-teleological opposition will, from the idea's point of
view, be striking. What an opposition, to keep to the higher representatives, does
the fern form against the acacia! The root, the stem, the leaf, the habit, the
whole exterior, the inner structure of every single part --- everything in this
juxtaposition is on each side marked by the fundamental form's typical peculiarity.
Since the spore lacks the polar opposition between upward and downward, the fern
has of course no main root and can have none; in the acacia, on the other hand,
there is, from germination's first moment on, a sharply expressed separation
between the original root and what is to become stem. The fern's stem --- often
perennial, tall and slender --- shows no small differentiation in the anatomical
respect (mighty vascular bundles surround the pith
% --- p. 302 ---
and so on); but since the fern-stem, when we look at the general case, brings
forth no branches shooting out above the earth, and since the terminal bud
consequently yields the living fronds alone, the cryptogams' high-placed
representative must after all lack in habit the branching so characteristic of the
plant shape. Such a branching, typical and rich, proceeds on the other hand from
the acacia's woody but yet richly articulated stem --- a stem with the highest
anatomical requisites: epidermis, bark, bast, sapwood, heartwood and pith. For a
cryptogam, the fern is amply furnished as regards leaf formation; the first frond
shooting out from the prothallium is indeed insignificant, but each following one
increases in strength, and usually in incision, until the highest vegetative
fullness is attained; that these fronds have stomata, and thus specially developed
conditions of respiration, is an unmistakable sign of the higher rank the fern
occupies among ``the flowerless''. But flowerless the fern is; that lies in its
cryptogamic nature: its highest productions are the spore-bearing fronds
(\textit{frondes fertiles}). On the phanerogamic stage, by contrast, leaf formation
attains a quite different development, both more many-sided and higher. In the
acacia the leaf is expressed in all its parts: the sheath is often divided into
stipules several inches long and perennial, the blade genuinely compound and in as
manifold ways as is otherwise rare in the plant kingdom; indeed the mobility of
form is here so great that the New Holland acacias, for example, often do not even
have developed leaf-blades but phyllodes, each formed of a leaf-stalk
(\textit{petiolus}), while the richest and most finely articulated leaf forms occur
for example in \textit{Mimosa pudica}. That the acacia must, in accordance with the
phanerogamic mode of propagation, have flower-leaves besides vegetative ones goes
without saying; the many-sidedness with which flower formation here proceeds
however particularly characterizes the summit on which the acacia stands. After
many oscillations through the irregular

% --- p. 303 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
(\textit{Papilionaceæ}) the regular is here attained in flower formation; the
regular hypogynous flower --- precisely the form in which the flower-individuals
step forward most distinctly --- has a very large but indeterminate number of
stamens; indeed the otherwise single fruit can in some forms
(\textit{Affonsia}) even be multiple: the many-sidedness is thus here in all parts
at its height\footnote{Within this family the two vital phenomena, sleep and
sensitivity (\textit{Mimosa sensitiva}, \textit{Smithia sensitiva}), are then also
very prominent.}.

``But'' --- one objects from empirical research's critical standpoint --- ``it is
after all a risky business with these nature-philosophical illuminations by the
idea; before one rightly knows it, the scientific standard has transformed itself
into a merely aesthetic one, and the poetic ideals of the imagination have put
colour on the prosaic facts''\footnote{Das Unternehmen, die Gattungen der Thiere
und Pflanzen in eine aufsteigende Stufenreihe zu ordnen, setzt nicht nur die
Kenntniß aller für beide Reiche charakteristischen Merkmale, sondern auch eine
mögliche Beurtheilung des Werthes voraus, den jedes einzelne besitzt. Die erste
dieser Aufgaben könnte vielleicht durch die aufgezählten mannigfachen Unterschiede
zwischen Thieren und Pflanzen erledigt scheinen, deren jeder zugleich einen
positiven Beitrag zu dem Bilde dessen ausmacht, das er von dem andern trennt; die
zweite ist unläugbar schwieriger, und die Entscheidung, welche Züge des thierischen
Daseins z. B. in einer Stufenleiter der Vollkommenheit immer deutlicher hervortreten
müßten, welche andere im Gegentheil durch ihr Zurückbleiben oder Verschwinden zur
Vervollkommnung beitragen, bleibt meist einer ästhetisch erregten Phantasie
überlassen, die in der Wahl ihrer Gesichtspunkte häufig fehlgreift. Diese
Uebelstände werden stets vorhanden sein, so lange die Begriffe von Pflanze und
Thier nur aus der Beobachtung gewonnen sind, die immer nur einen gewissen
Thatbestand der Verbindung und Gruppirung von anschaulichen Elementen nachweist,
ohne uns die Form oder die Gleichung, durch welche Werth und Beziehung derselben
unter einander bestimmt wird, neben der anschaulichen Erscheinung noch einmal
ausdrücklich zu wiederholen.'' [The undertaking of ordering the genera of animals
and plants into an ascending series of stages presupposes not only knowledge of all
the marks characteristic of both kingdoms, but also a possible assessment of the
worth each single one possesses. The first of these tasks might perhaps seem
settled by the manifold differences enumerated between animals and plants, each of
which at the same time makes a positive contribution to the image of the one it
separates from the other; the second is undeniably harder, and the decision as to
which traits of animal existence would have to come forward ever more distinctly in
a ladder of perfection, and which others on the contrary contribute to perfecting
by their receding or disappearing, is mostly left to an aesthetically excited
imagination, which frequently goes wrong in the choice of its points of view. These
drawbacks will always be present so long as the concepts of plant and animal are
gained from observation alone, which only ever demonstrates a certain state of
affairs in the connection and grouping of intuitable elements, without expressly
repeating for us once more, alongside the intuitable appearance, the form or the
equation by which their worth and relation to one another is determined.] Lotze.
Allgem. Physiol. pp. 507--8.}. Before we go into
% (QUOTE BALANCE, as printed in the Danish: the German quotation in this note
%  opens WITHOUT a mark but is closed with one at „wiederholen.“ on p. 304 ---
%  a missing opening quote in the setting, left uncorrected. Mirrored here.)
% --- p. 304 ---
% (the foot of this page in the Danish carries the run-over of the p. 303
%  note **), given above at its anchor; no mark and no counter reset here.)
an answering of the questions belonging under this, we shall, in order to win the
requisite presuppositions, make use of the information essential for an assessment
of the fundamental forms which still offers itself from another side.

\emph{ββ) Metamorphoses.} Were it possible that fern-fronds could be found on an
acacia tree, and acacia leaves on a fern-stem; were it possible that the acacia
could bear alternately both flowers and spores, and the fern again alternately
spores and flowers --- yes, if such a thing were possible, then the teleological
fundamental form would doubtless be a fantasy, but then botany as a science would
be a fantasy too. That the different formations belonging to a determinate organism
proceed from one disposition and constitute a teleological system is seen from
metamorphosis.

In his famous work ``Die Metamorphose der Pflanzen'' Goethe expressed the
epoch-making fundamental thought in botany that all the different parts of a
phanerogamic plant, from the cotyledon to the pistil, come forth in this way, that
one and the same element clothes itself at the different heights of the axis in a
different shape and thus presents itself as transformed. By manifold comparisons
--- by comparing leaves on the same plant at different heights, by comparing the
leaves lying on either side with the intervening leaf of monstrous constitution
(anamorphosis), and finally by comparing leaves of the same order at different
stages in the plant kingdom --- the intuitive genius made the discovery that what
at one stage was seed-leaf (cotyledon) was at another a foliage leaf, at a third a
bract, at a fourth a sepal, at a fifth a petal, at a sixth a stamen, at a seventh a
carpel. --- If a further proof is still demanded that the plant's parts do not come
forth by finite

% --- p. 305 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
piecing together but are developed from within out of one self-differentiating
disposition, so that the organs' difference and mutually determined relation is
essentially grounded on the paradigm's teleological demands, then we need only
recall the way in which Schleiden in particular has tested and carried through what
Goethe intuited. Instead of merely comparing the phenomena in their immediate
existence, Schleiden asks at the same time after their becoming, goes back to the
original, compares for example a cotyledon in its first appearance with the first
appearances of other leaves on the same plant, and finds then that all the leaves
resemble each other from the beginning, that they are all originally of one form,
each of them ``a wart-like protrusion below the tip of the
axis\footnote{%
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish: "Tet" as printed for "Det" --- a wrong
%  sort, the capital being the same T as in "er Tale" three words later.
%  Not reproducible in English and so logged only.)
It must be expressly brought out that in botany there is no question of
metamorphosed leaves in the same sense as when in geognosy one speaks of
metamorphosed stones. That marble and alabaster are formed by metamorphosis
presupposes that the marble was simple limestone before it became marble, and the
alabaster gypsum before it became alabaster; but a filament is not a metamorphosed
petal in the sense that it should really have been a petal before it became a
filament. ``So lange man jedoch Stengel und Blatt nur nach ihrem weniger
morphologischen als vielmehr descriptiv geometrischen Begriffe unterscheidet, ist
es schwer zu sagen, ob in der Ansicht, welche verschiedene Theile der Blüthe als
Umwandlungen des Blattes betrachtet, mehr Tautologie oder mehr unklare Mystik
liegt.'' [So long, however, as one distinguishes stem and leaf only according to
their less morphological than rather descriptively geometrical concept, it is hard
to say whether there lies more tautology or more obscure mysticism in the view
which regards different parts of the flower as transformations of the leaf.] Lotze.
Allgem. Physiol. p. 535.}.''

In so far as what is originally of like kind is, each in its place and according to
its determination, transformed into something of unlike kind, the idea-cause rules;
in so far as the idea-cause, the teleological principle, rules, a parallelism
between the organs' connection in the single plant and the types' connection in the
plant kingdom's ``natural system'' is unmistakable. Here there is then not merely a
metamorphosis of organs but also a metamorphosis of fundamental

% --- p. 306 ---
forms; if we call the former the real, we must call the latter the ideal: both have
at bottom the same originality, the same essential validity. If the real
metamorphosis signifies that the different organs on the same organism are
transformations of one and the same fundamental element, then the ideal
metamorphosis signifies that the separate types are all transformations, and just
thereby repetitions, of one and the same fundamental type. The difference, that
between the organs on the same organism a material connection takes place while
between the different types no such corresponding physical reciprocal action lets
itself be pointed out, may indeed not be overlooked; but everywhere that the
idea-cause holds, the ideal connection has greater reality than the merely material
one: on the ideal connection's original reality the parallelism between the real and
the ideal metamorphosis is grounded. A true insight into this parallelism is
conditioned by true insight into the dialectical unity of the thing-causes, the
physical intermediate causes, with the fundamental cause.

The speculative philosophy of nature, which abstracts all too easily from the
physical intermediate causes, takes the idea's name in vain and misuses the
parallelism, in that it gives itself over, in order to follow the upward-striding
movement, to the guidance of an arbitrary aestheticizing intuition; the critical
research which denies the idea-cause and regards the parallelism merely as a work
of a comparing abstraction takes the fact's name in vain and can, for all its many
pieces of information about ``the material of formation'', about ``carbonic acid''
and ``ammonia'', about ``cellulose'' and ``albumin'' and ``matter's plastic force''
and ``the physical conditions'', and so on, scarcely even find its way in its own
hypotheses, let alone in the teleological forms.

``But'' --- one objects from critical research's empirical standpoint --- ``what
use is it to appeal to the idea-cause and the teleological fundamental form when
one
% --- p. 307 ---
in reality can neither demonstrate how the physical causes in particular hang
together with the fundamental cause, nor explain the characteristic marks'
mutually necessary connection out of what is teleological in the fundamental form?
That between the fern's root, stem and leaves there is an essential connection no
one will deny; the organic reciprocal action entails precisely that such a stem
must have such leaves, and conversely. That the fern organism's peculiarity lets
itself be referred to the causes co-operating in its propagation must likewise be
conceded; but what is the teleological now to explain, what use has one for the
idea-cause? That the fern cannot have flowers when propagation happens by spores,
and cannot have a main root when the spore lacks polarity, goes without saying;
but from what teleology could it be derived that ferns in our climate are
herbaceous plants, while the woody stems with which they often appear in tropical
regions have so predominant a likeness to palm stems --- and that although palms
belong to the flowering plants? Or is there perhaps some particular idea-cause
from which one can explain why the fern's leaf must absolutely have its youngest
part at the tip, whereas plants' leaves otherwise are, as Schleiden has shown,
morphologically bounded by having --- in opposition to the axis, the morphologically
unbounded --- typically the oldest part at the tip? Can one by any illumination
from the idea discover the ground of the fact that fertilization must in the ferns'
case, precisely because such a cryptogamic organism is to be formed here, take
place on the prothallium, while in the mosses it takes place on the plant itself,
although the mosses are cryptogamic too?''

``That the vascular bundles in the phanerogamic stem are now scattered and lack a
cambial ring, now on the contrary gathered, set in a whorl and in alternating
relation to the cambial ring, is a fact; but where then do we find the idea which
tells us whence it

% --- p. 308 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
really comes that it is just the dicotyledonous plants that have cambial rings,
not the monocotyledonous? So long as one takes the matter quite generally and
keeps it hovering, one can doubtless very easily uphold the idea's validity; but on
going into particulars and looking at the facts one strikes everywhere on one of
two cases: either the connection between a fundamental form's typical marks lets
itself be explained by
% (letterspacing in the Danish covers ONLY „physiske“ and „physiologiske“;
%  „anatomiske“ and „morphologiske“ are set normally.)
\emph{physical} --- \emph{physiological}, anatomical, morphological ---
causes\footnote{In how many ways gravity alone is able to bring about
characteristic alterations in the plant organism, the newest investigations
belonging here prove. ``Bei Untersuchung der Mechanik der geocentrischen
Krümmungen pflanzlicher Organe ist vor Allem der Umstand scharf im Auge zu
behalten, daß von gewißen verschiedenen Organen völlig gleichen anatomischen Baues
die einen bei Einwirkung der Schwerkraft aufwärts, die anderen abwärts sich
krümmen. Sichtbare Unterschiede der Structur eines Stengels und einer Wurzel von
\textit{Nitella}, eines auf- und eines abwärts wachsenden Sproßes von
\textit{Equisetum palustre} sind nicht vorhanden; die Differenzen der Anordnung und
Vertheilung der Gewebe in Stengel und Wurzel derselben Pflanze sind häufig
verschwindend klein. Wohl aber zeigen Stengel und Wurzel in ihren geocentrischer
Krümmung fähigen Theilen ein sehr verschiedenes Maaß von Differenzen der Spannung
der einzelnen Gewebsmassen.'' [In investigating the mechanics of the geocentric
curvatures of plant organs, the circumstance above all is to be kept sharply in
view that of certain different organs of wholly like anatomical structure some
curve upward under the action of gravity and others downward. Visible differences
in the structure of a stem and a root of Nitella, of an upward- and a
downward-growing shoot of Equisetum palustre, are not present; the differences in
the arrangement and distribution of the tissues in stem and root of the same plant
are frequently vanishingly small. But stem and root do show, in those of their
parts capable of geocentric curvature, a very different measure of differences in
the tension of the single tissue-masses.] W. Hofmeister. Ueber die durch die
Schwerkraft bestimmten Richtungen von Pflanzentheilen. Jahrbücher für
wissenschaftliche Botanik von Pringsheim. Vol. III. Part 1. Berlin 1861. p. 77 and
following.}, and then the idea-cause is quite superfluous; or else such physical
causes do not let themselves be pointed out, and then it is only empty talk when
one, to conceal one's ignorance, invokes the teleological and appeals to a
meaningless idea-cause.''

It is the old ingrained misunderstanding --- that an idea-cause is nothing and
signifies nothing when it is not there as a physical element (and so cannot be
glimpsed under the microscope either) --- which has here expressed itself in new
turns of phrase. So

% --- p. 309 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
long as this misunderstanding rules, even the most acute research will be caught in
the contradiction of having to acknowledge nature and yet deny nature in one
breath: nature is idea; its ``formative drive'', its ``plastic force'', its
``typical forms'' are the idea's utterances, the idea-cause's effects.

If we consider, just to have something determinate in view, the diagnosis by which
botanists distinguish the red and the blue Anagallis\footnote{Cf.\ J. Lange,
Haandb. i den danske Flora. Copenhagen 1856--59. p. 165.}, then there shows itself
here the most striking likeness: ``stem outstretched'', ``leaves opposite'',
``calyx-lobes awl-shaped'' and so on, united with an equally striking unlikeness;
for in the first (\textit{A. arvensis}) ``the corolla is red'', in the last
(\textit{A. coerulea}) ``the corolla is blue''. It is on the whole a fact which
repeats itself under many alterations in the plant kingdom, that a striking
unlikeness in some parts is found united with a remarkable likeness in
others\footnote{A striking mutual likeness have, as regards the grasses,
\textit{Poa nemoralis} and \textit{Poa fertilis}; but with this likeness ---
``culm round, even'', ``panicle with rough branches, erect after flowering'',
``spikelets 2--5 flowered'' --- there unites an equally determinate unlikeness,
inasmuch as ``the leaf-sheaths'' in the former are ``shorter than the corresponding
internodes, the uppermost shorter than its leaf-blade; the ligule short, truncate'',
--- in the latter: ``the leaf-sheaths of equal length with the internodes, the
uppermost of equal length with its blade; the ligule elongated''; --- in the former
``the panicle is narrow'', in the latter ``spreading''; in the former ``the paleas
are pointed'', in the latter ``blunt''. See J. Lange. Work cited. pp. 79--80.

If we compare the common strawberry (\textit{Fragaria vesca}) with the so-called
hill strawberry (\textit{F. collina}), the likeness between the two is in all parts
so pervasive that the latter differs from the former (cf.\ J. Lange. pp. 353--54)
only in this, that its ``calyx at maturity'' is ``pressed close to the dark
rose-red, sweet fruit''.}; so

% --- p. 310 ---
likewise can manifold crucifers exhibit great likeness as regards flower and stem
leaves while the siliques are typically unlike; so likewise one finds not merely
within each class and each order, but in each family and in each genus, the like
combined in the most different directions with the unlike: what does this now
signify?

It signifies that two plants which in one single point are typically unlike each
other must in all organic points be mutually unlike; it signifies that the unlike
within the likeness rests on an ideal metamorphosis. That the corolla in the one
specimen is blue and in the other red shows that the organic activity in the one
corolla is different from the corresponding activity in the other; but the organic
functions in the corolla stand, according to the organism's concept, in the most
intimate reciprocal action with the functions in the calyx, in the bracts, in the
stem leaves --- in a word, in all the plant's remaining parts. The apparently like
must thus after all be an unlike, since in a single organ it brings forth something
unlike; and those so unlike in the phenomenon must have essential likeness, since
they come forth in organic connection with all the rest that is like. The red and
the blue Anagallis, which particularly with respect to the corolla's colour are so
unlike each other that one has in consequence been able to assume a difference of
species, are moreover in all else so mutually alike that the latter can even be
regarded as a variety of the former. Here then, as regards the corollas, there is
not simply unlikeness in a single respect and for the rest likeness in all else;
but the unlikeness between the corollas presupposes an unlikeness pervading all the
remaining parts, however difficult it may be to point it out in the phenomenon, and
the likeness in all the rest presupposes in turn that the unlikeness in the
corollas' colour must rest on an alteration of something otherwise common, and thus
be after all grounded in likeness.

The fern and the palm are not like each other in some respects and unlike in
others; they are in all essen%
% --- p. 311 ---
tial points like each other, although in all points they are yet unlike each other.
If the fern were not in all essential points identical with the palm, and if this
identity did not have to hold literally, then the plant could not be plant. For
what is the fern other than plant, and what is the palm other than plant? But if
the plant's identity with itself as fern and as palm were not in all points
determined by difference and opposition, then the fern would have to be palm and
the palm fern; that the same being, the same plant, has become two beings, two
mutually heterogeneous plants --- in that the metamorphosis is seen. Here then,
according to the metamorphosis, there is not a partial identity alternating with a
partial difference, but an identity determined by the difference, a difference
grounded in the identity: the fern is not in these and those parts --- in the
spores, for example --- a cryptogam, and in other parts --- in the leaves, for
example, or in the stem's vascular bundles --- a phanerogam; no, the fern is in all
parts and parts of parts cryptogamic, just as the palm is in all points and parts
phanerogamic.

Only by fixing the gaze on the idea is it possible to get comparison's and
abstraction's misdirections corrected, possible to grasp the metamorphosis. So
long as the thing-causes furnish cognition's highest presuppositions, the identity
of organic identity and organic opposition will always present itself as a
mystification of concepts. Far from seeing the fern's and the palm's punctual
difference, expressed through every particular, reflected in the palm's concrete
unity of essence with the fern, critical understanding will --- so long as it has
the splinter of finitude in its eye, that is, so long as the physical intermediate
causes have not yet been transformed into moments of the idea --- constantly help
itself with ``a partial likeness'' and ``a partial difference'', with ``likeness in
something'' and ``unlikeness in something else'', with ``community in the general''
and

% --- p. 312 ---
``peculiarity in the particular''; so long as thinking has not worked its way
through to the category of metamorphosis, and thereby to insight into the general's
ideal-material concretion with the particular and the single, it will be an
impossibility to acknowledge the plant's interior, the fundamental form itself, for
more than an abstraction.

That with cognition a transformation, an essential alteration, takes place when
the physical causes are grasped as moments in the idea-cause is unmistakable. In
the opposition of cause and effect we have then, from the idea's point of view, a
real, effective opposition between the system of causal determinations in its
inner, ideal totality on the one side and the corresponding effects, determined by
the articulation of matter, in their real totality on the other. If the emphasis
now falls on the opposition thus carried through, then each of the terms --- the
plant itself and the plant's idea, the fern and the fern's idea, the palm and the
palm's idea --- lays claim in equal degree to actuality. For it cannot be the idea
for itself that is immediately the actual, since the idea's actualization happens
precisely by way of material existence; still less can material existence for
itself appropriate actuality's priority, since it does not bring itself forth but
is realized by the idea realizing itself therein: the idea's and the plant's
actuality is one and the same; here there are not two but one actual.

What then is the idea? The actual plant! And the plant? The actual idea! The
identity in which the opposition is now resolved furnishes a corrective which
scientific cognition cannot possibly do without. For the sensuous-understanding
consideration, immediate existence --- the existing plant --- presents itself as
the positive, as what exists in actuality; therefore sound sense says: ``I see the
plant well enough, but the plant's idea I do not see.'' In this sound sense is so
far perfectly right, since it is not

% --- p. 313 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
the immediately positive that is the idea. If the immediately positive were the
idea, then the animal would have to eat the idea when it eats the plant; then one
would also be able to tear the idea apart when one tears the plant apart, to boil
the idea when one boils the plant, and so on; if the immediately positive were the
absolutely positive, then the idea would be without reality and critical
understanding would be in the right. But this is precisely the corrective
indispensable for cognition: that what presents itself to sensuous beholding, the
immediately positive, is not the positive true itself; that what is beholdable in
its phenomenal immediacy is precisely so perishable, so fleeting, because it is
essentially the negative, the non-independent which serves another --- the matter
in which ideality burns, the semblance in which the idea shines.

Not by the destruction of immediate existence, of the existing plant, and its
giving place to other equally immediate existences, other plants, but by the
sensuous in existence, the immediately positive, presenting itself as the
transparent in reflection --- the negative which essentially confirms the positive
resulting from the negated negation\footnote{Cf.\ Phil. Propædeutik.
pp. 152---58.}, the idea itself, the teleologically active --- can truth be
cognized.

\emph{γγ) Individuations.} As existing organism --- as fern, as palm, as willow
and so on --- the plant presents itself to immediate consideration in the shape of
a natural individuality; but where in this individuality is the real individual?
If one understands by the individual the organic formation which has the capacity
either itself to subsist as an independent organism or at least to bring forth
such a one, one easily discovers that the plant, the vegetating specimen, is not a
single individual but a brood

% --- p. 314 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
of individuals. Every shoot (\textit{turio}), every bud (\textit{gemma}), every
leaf\footnote{Alexander Braun. Das Individuum der Pflanze. Berlin 1853.} --- indeed
even a part of a leaf --- can under favourable conditions yield a real
presupposition for an independently growing plant. Even if one goes back with
Schleiden to the cell, one still does not find the absolute individual, since a
single-celled alga, a \textit{Caulerpa} for example, can be torn in two and the
parts still grow.

The question of the plant individual and its reality is here of so much the more
decisive significance as there seems to hide under this question a serious tension
between the paradigmatic law of selfhood and the law of propagation. --- ``On the
tendency which in the more recent investigations makes itself more and more felt,
this leaf which composes the whole plant, and which continually transformed steps
forward in more and more perfect forms on the plant, can no longer be regarded as a
single instrument or ``organ'' in the sense in which one of our limbs or organs is
so, but is a self-enclosed whole and unit, with the whole plant's nature in it. In
comparison with such individual plants or plant-units, the whole plant is a
co-growth, an organic community of leaves which according to inner harmony strive
each to its own end, and yet all to a common and higher one, on which the welfare
of each single one depends. This consideration of the leaf as a unit and of the
plant as a state I regard as the only natural one, and as the only one which
permits us to see plant life in its right likeness and unlikeness to animal life
. . . .
% (the ellipsis is set as four spaced points in the Danish, as printed)
\emph{But if the leaves are real units, then the stamens and the carpels are also
in their good right as such, and the two sexes are as little united here as
elsewhere in one being.}'' (Steenstrup\footnote{Unders. over Hermaphroditismen.
pp. 85---86.}).

% --- p. 315 ---
Critically the matter now stands thus. If one rejects --- as there is here surely
every possible reason to do --- the doctrine of hermaphroditism, then one must
also consistently concede that stamens and carpels are individuals of opposite
sex; but when the plant in its wholeness, instead of being a single individual, is
an organic community, a state of individuals, what then becomes of the paradigmatic
unity, of the selfhood articulating itself in the whole system? If one grasps ---
from the other side, where there is surely also every possible reason for it ---
the whole plant as a living unity and reduces the alleged leaf-individuals to
moments in its individuality, then a decisive emphasis falls indeed on the
paradigmatic selfhood penetrating the manifold members; but when the single leaves,
on this way of seeing, cease to be ``plant-units'', how do we then avoid breaking
the law of propagation and falling back into hermaphroditism? The expedient of
letting the one paradigm answering to the whole plant dissolve into a system of
separate paradigms is far too risky. If every leaf is really to represent an a
priori independent selfhood, then every cell --- indeed presumably every cell
membrane and every drop of cell sap --- must likewise presuppose its separate
paradigm, its a priori independent selfhood; but when such a consequence is carried
through, what is the whole explanation with paradigm and selfhood and disposition
and preformations other than an empty shadow-play, a metaphysical play of thought?

If one of two things held unconditionally --- namely that the plant in its wholeness
must be either a living unity or a living plurality, either an absolute total
individual or a composition of absolutely independent partial individuals --- then
there would undeniably be a nodal point here. But the either-or thus set up has no
validity. The partial individual is in our ``organic state'' just as little
absolute as the total individual. The emphasis falls here, on whichever side one
may

% --- p. 316 ---
seek the individual, not so much on the self-enclosed existence of the
``plant-units'' as on their peculiar working and the activity in the course of which they are formed
--- thus: not so much on the individuals as on the individuation. But individuation
goes, when we see it in the idea's light, in two mutually opposed directions, a
centrifugal and a centripetal. In virtue of the centrifugal striving the unity
becomes means for the plurality; the total individual acquires from this side a
significance as ``a co-growth'', as ``the organic state'' in which the partial
individuals are independent citizens. In virtue of the centripetal striving the
plurality conversely becomes means for the unity; the vegetating citizens are from
this side only the state's vegetative organs, real moments in which the articulated
whole reflects its original unity.

Which of these directions of individuation is to be regarded as the superordinate
will in each single case depend on how far the task in question demands that
express weight be laid either on the single functions in their separateness or on
the fundamental function, the unity of function determining the whole system. If
the matter in hand is thus a ``law of propagation'' which is ``expressed
everywhere in living nature'', excluding hermaphroditism, then the matter in hand
is expressly one of life's separate functions, a function conditioned precisely by a quite separate
opposition; here there is then assuredly every reason to lay the emphasis on the
separate and to see the opposite sexes represented by opposite individuals. If the
question is on the other hand about selfhood's reality, about the paradigm's a
priori validity, then the general function pervading the functions must necessarily
become the superordinate, and the single functions in their plurality and
separateness the subordinate; it follows then from the nature of the case that the
whole emphasis must fall on the unity itself, the unity localizing itself in the
whole, the unity of principle.

% --- p. 317 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
Instead of a single absolute individual or a co-growth of absolute individuals, we
have then in the plant a \emph{system of organic individuations.} This system then
gives us, in so far as it comprises the metamorphoses characteristic of the
particular plant's whole course of life, the plant's natural individuality, the
shape in which the paradigm is realized\footnote{From an aesthetic point of view
the higher-standing plant will of course always make the impression of being a
branched natural individual. --- ``So stellen sich denn von der Wurzel bis zur
Frucht eine Reihe bedeutsamer an die Innigkeit anklingender Gestalten hin, welche
jedoch erst dadurch zu ihrer wahren Bedeutung gelangen, daß sie als das Ganze des
Pflauzenindividuums constituirende Momente erfaßt werden. Denn so wie die
Stimmungen erst dann in ihrer Wahrheit erkannt werden, wenn man sie auf die
Einheit der Innigkeit zurückführt, so erhalten auch die Zustände der
Pflanzengestaltung erst dann ihren wahren Charakter, wenn sie zum Einen Ganzen,
zur Physiognomie des Pflanzenindividuums zusammenstimmen. Für sich genommen, mag
jede Stimmung und jeder Zustand des Reizendeu Manches enthalten, die Wahrheit des
Lebens offenbart sich aber doch erst in und aus der Geschlossenheit des
Individuums''. [Thus there present themselves, from the root to the fruit, a series
of significant shapes echoing inwardness, which however first attain their true
significance by being grasped as moments constituting the whole of the plant
individual. For just as moods are first cognized in their truth when one leads them
back to the unity of inwardness, so too do the states of plant formation first
receive their true character when they accord together into one whole, into the
physiognomy of the plant individual. Taken for itself, each mood and each state of
the charming may contain much; but the truth of life reveals itself only in and out
of the self-enclosedness of the individual.]
% (PRINTER'S DEFECTS reproduced, the German being verbatim: „Pflauzenindividuums“
%  at its first occurrence and „des Reizendeu“ --- two turned n's in this one
%  note. The second occurrence of the word reads „Pflanzenindividuums“ correctly.
F. Th. Bratranek. Beitr. z. e. Æsthet. der Pflanzw. p. 224. --- Cf.\
A. S. Ørsted, Et Bidrag til Træernes Architektonik. Tidsskr. for popul. Fremstill.
af Naturvidskb. 2nd series. Vol. IV. Copenhagen 1862. p. 78 and following.}. In
this shape the unity-in-difference of the whole and its parts, the inner and the
outer, the total individual and its partial individuals, is then also --- according
to what we have seen in the foregoing --- many-sidedly determined by the articulated
opposition of selfhood and otherness, in so far as these factors of the opposition
abidingly maintain each its own validity in the system of functions and step forward
each as a peculiar power.

If we ask which of the two powers is the stronger, selfhood or otherness, a
dialectic of power and powerlessness will show itself on both sides. Essentially
understood, selfhood --- which in principle bears otherness within itself --- is
the original, the higher, and so far the more powerful; otherness, which has

% --- p. 318 ---
what integrates it outside itself, is on the contrary the derived, the lower, and
so far the powerless, the weaker. But if the emphasis now falls, as actuality
likewise demands, on the phenomenon, the factual, the immediacy that strikes one
in the fact, then the relation turns about: then otherness, which has its strength
precisely in the outwardly immediate, must necessarily show itself as the original,
the stronger --- and that not merely although but just because it is what is by its
essence derived, the weaker; likewise must selfhood, whose validity rests on
reflective energy, necessarily present itself as the derived, the abstracted, a
faint shading-off in subjective reflection --- and that not merely although but
just because it is what is at bottom itself the original, the eternal and
imperishable in objective reflection.

In the vegetating plant --- a bulb, for example --- life shows itself as a
remarkable ambiguity, an infinite reciprocal determination of actual and unactual.
This wealth of flowers and these juicy green leaves with their millions of cells,
whose constituents are fetched from water and air, give us a properly vivid and
colourful image of the principle's omnipresent working, of the superiority with
which selfhood conquers the atoms' elementary otherness, of that executive power
of ideality which at every point satisfies the paradigm's teleological demands.
But just as we involuntarily rejoice at having this expressed image of selfhood
before our eyes, and step closer properly to observe the master-workman revealing
himself in the beautiful work, the ideality is suddenly gone. The idea vanishes
like a phantom; the ideally actual transforms itself into an unactual. One can
disturb the idea's work; one can tear off the flowers, tear the leaves apart;
indeed it goes even more easily to tear these juicy green leaves apart and break
this brittle stem than to tear a cord apart or break a glass: ``if the living
plant is really one with the idea realizing itself
% (the quotation opened here closes on p. 319, at „…dens Værk!“)
% --- p. 319 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
therein, then'' --- one says --- ``the idea must indeed be a powerless shadow-being,
since one can with so little trouble annihilate its work!''\footnote{``Wollten wir
irgend eine Idee als die wirkende Macht ansehen, die in einer Erscheinung thätig
ist, so wäre wenigstens dies gewiß, daß wir uns zu einer unthätigen Stellung ihr
gegenüber verurtheilten. Denn Ideen der Natur zu verändern, ihre Wirkungen
aufzuheben oder zu begünstigen, besitzen wir doch wohl kein Mittel, wir müßten
denn den Sinn dieser Ansicht entschlossen so vervollständigen, daß wir uns
solchen Mächten geistiger Art mit ebenso geistreichen Beschwörungen und
Zauberformeln gegenüberstellten.'' [Were we to regard any idea as the effective
power at work in an appearance, this at least would be certain, that we should
condemn ourselves to an inactive position over against it. For to alter ideas of
nature, to cancel or to favour their effects, we surely possess no means --- unless
we were resolutely to complete the sense of this view by setting ourselves over
against such powers of a spiritual kind with equally spirited conjurations and
magic formulas.] Lotze. Allgem. Physiol. p. 36. --- This way of seeing can be
summed up briefly in these words: if nature were not without idea, human beings
could not by natural means act upon its productions. From this it follows at the
same time that there can be no active reason in nature either; for if nature really
is without idea, it must also be without reason.} But the power of reflection is
one thing and the power of immediacy another.

In accordance with the vegetative organism's natural unity with the idea, the
plant kingdom is to be grasped as a kingdom of ideas realized on earth, stamped in
material forms. To gain an insight into this kingdom's organization it will be
necessary to investigate how far ``the natural system's'' species, genera,
families, orders, classes and series let themselves be cognized as ideas of ideas,
individuations of one fundamental idea.

The individual idea --- the paradigm actualized in the specimen --- is not an
unconditioned and abstract single being but a living concrete unity which reflects
its subsistence in a living plurality\footnote{Everywhere that the talk is of the
dialectical unity-in-difference of the specimen and the individual idea, it must be
expressly held fast that the specimen must so far be congruent with the idea as its
existence is only a material expression for the system of articulating
determinations which make up selfhood's paradigmatic content. The absurdity which
always shows itself when one will in an altogether immediate way carry over the
categories of sensuousness to the ideal cannot then surprise us in this connection.
Meanwhile --- for literary bestiality is often, where one least expects it, at hand
with ``contributions to a critique'' --- a couple of examples of clumsy
misunderstandings might perhaps be in place here. ``If'' --- so one can doubtless
reason from ``the critique's'' standpoint --- ``a willow tree's individual idea
coincides with the actual willow tree, then one tears off a piece of the individual
idea when one tears off a willow twig. If the one willow specimen of the same
species can be now smaller, now larger than the other, then the one corresponding
individual idea must also be able to be now smaller, now larger than the other; the
ideas must thus be able to grow and increase like the specimens. But if an
individual idea grows with its growing specimen, then it must also wither with its
withering, rot with its rotting specimen, and so on.'' Now when one has by that
sort of ``contribution to a critique'' got it proved in a loud voice --- and thus
proved thoroughly --- that the specimen cannot possibly present any individual
idea, since on so absurd a presupposition one would by cutting through the specimen
absolutely have to cut through the idea, then one need only draw a consequence which
reminds ``the critique'' that a sphere's centre of gravity can have no reality
either (Mathem. og Dialekt. pp. 129---30), since on so absurd a presupposition one
would literally have to \emph{cleave the centre of gravity} when one halved the
sphere.}. If the individual idea's existence goes up in

% --- p. 320 ---
% (the second note of p. 319 (**) runs over onto this page in the Danish,
%  filling the lower half; it is given whole at its anchor above, so no mark
%  and no counter reset here.)
this way into the corresponding existence of the specimen, then it goes without
saying that the paradigm \textit{in concreto} must, in its own way, be just as
individual as the individual specimen. Here then there is not a manifold of
specimens in which one and the same paradigm has repeated itself; such a general
paradigm is only a schema, an abstract form, not an idea: the individual idea
cannot repeat itself. If one nevertheless thinks to see a repetition of the
individual idea in the fact that the one paradigm so absolutely resembles the
other, then one must also regard the specimens resembling each other in everything
essential as repetitions of one and the same speci%

% --- p. 321 ---
men. Now if it is a contradiction that one and the same specimen should be able to
exist as a plurality of specimens, then it is also a contradiction that one and the
same individual idea should exist as a plurality of ideas. The many mutually
resembling specimens come forth by just as many mutually like acts of propagation;
the many mutually like acts of propagation presuppose just as many mutually like
principles of selfhood; the many mutually like principles of selfhood are
conditioned concentrations of one and the same, in itself unconditioned,
fundamental principle: only so is an existential repetition thinkable.

``But'' --- someone might ask --- ``is it then not just as absurd that one and the
same principle should by existential repetition be able to become a plurality of
principles, as that one and the same idea should by an existence repeated several
times become a plurality of ideas?'' The answering of such a question is not
difficult. That the same principle of existence should, either at once or
gradually, go up and work in a plurality of existing things is --- so long as the
decisive emphasis falls on ``existence'' and ``existing'' --- quite certainly an
insoluble contradiction; existence rests on the \textit{principium exclusi medii}:
either \textit{a} or \textit{b}; the one existing thing excludes the other. When
the existing things thus relate exclusively to one another, then the principles of
existence must doubtless likewise relate exclusively to one another, in so far as
they are each actualized in corresponding existences. But just as every existence
with inner necessity presupposes its own particular ground, so must \emph{every
principle of existence in corresponding fashion presuppose its fundamental
principle.} The particular existing things relate exclusively in existence; but
the particular grounds by no means relate exclusively in the ground. On the
contrary! the particular grounds come forth precisely, on given conditions,

% --- p. 322 ---
from one ground common to them all, the unity of ground, the general ground. Only
in the unity of ground, the general ground, reflecting itself through the
conditions in the particular grounds, do we have the ground necessary and
sufficient for existence (\textit{ratio sufficiens}).

In the infinite reflection of activity which lets the unconditioned penetrate the
conditions, the same now holds of the principles. In that the organic principles
of existence, by their identity with the existing organisms, the sensuously given
specimens, separate exclusively in existence, they show as particular fundamental
causes at the same time their identity with the ground, and are thus in all their
unconditionedness conditioned effects of the unity of principle, the fundamental
principle, reflected in them all. Since now the individual idea is nothing other
than the principle of existence articulated in the organism --- \emph{the inner
organism, energetically reflected in its material outer,} more closely determined
as absolute unity-in-difference of means and purpose --- it is understood from this
that the individual ideas must in their existential separation absolutely be ideas
of one and the same fundamental idea. This fundamental idea realizing itself in the
individual ideas is now \emph{the species}.

What a difference it makes whether we grasp the species as an originally active
real idea or merely as a form, a concept abstracted by comparison of the specimens,
is striking: only a superficial thinking can confuse the living species with the
empty schema. If we say that all globular clouds are of the same kind, then we
grasp the concept of kind as schema; for the typical form is, as regards cloud
formation, not principle but result. It is the likeness predominating in the many
results of formation that we abstract as just so many mutually like forms; these
many mutually independent forms, indifferent to one another, then look in the
abstraction as though they were one single form grounding the single formations, a
fundamental form. Such a form is a dead abstractum; how superficial to regard the
living species

% --- p. 323 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
in the same way! In the living species the activity of form is essentially activity
of principle; the peculiar form in which the species as species expresses its
existence is originally determined by disposition and preformation. The form
preformed in the disposition must, in order to be the species' form, in principle
be differentiated into separate individual forms: \emph{only by way of the
individual idea can the species come to existence with the existing specimen.}

In so far as the individual ideas are of the same species, they must not only
resemble each other in essential points but --- what is here particularly to be
brought out --- be absolutely alike in all respects. If now all the individual
ideas of the plant world were absolutely alike, then the ideal concretion would
have to stop at the species as deepest fundamental idea. The manifold of specimens
on earth might then be as great as it liked; the whole wealth would nevertheless
consist in a merely outward superfluity: the concretion could go no further. Should
anyone make against this the objection that two ideas cannot possibly be absolutely
alike without their necessarily melting together and becoming one according to the
\textit{principium indiscernibilium}, the answer is that a difference of existence
can very well subsist along with a complete likeness\footnote{Der Satz des
Nichtzuunterscheidenden gründete sich eigentlich auf die Voraussetzung, daß, wenn
in dem Begriffe von einem Dinge überhaupt eine gewisse Unterscheidung nicht
angetroffen wird, sie auch nicht in den Dingen selbst anzutreffen sei; folglich
seien alle Dinge völlig einerlei (\textit{numero eadem}), die sich nicht schon in
ihrem Begriffe (der Qvalität oder Qvantität nach) von einander unterscheiden. Weil
aber bei dem blossen Begriffe von irgend einem Dinge von manchen nothwendigen
Bedingungen einer Anschauung abstrahirt worden, so wird durch eine sonderbare
Uebereilung das, wovon abstrahirt wird, dafür genommen, daß es überall nicht
anzutreffen sei, und dem Dinge nichts eingeräumt, als was in seinem Begriffe
enthalten ist. [The principle of the indiscernible was really grounded on the
presupposition that if a certain distinction is not met with in the concept of a
thing at all, it is not to be met with in the things themselves either;
consequently all things are wholly identical (numero eadem) which do not already
differ from one another in their concept (as to quality or quantity). But because in
the mere concept of any thing abstraction has been made from many necessary
conditions of an intuition, that from which abstraction is made is by a singular
overhastiness taken to be nowhere to be met with, and nothing is granted to the
thing but what is contained in its concept.] Kant. Von der Amphibolie der
Reflexionsbegriffe. Krit. d. r. Vern. p. 268 and following.},
% (as printed in the Danish: „Qvalität oder Qvantität“, with the Danish Qv for
%  Kant's „Qualität“/„Quantität“.)
% --- p. 324 ---
and that it is precisely the difference of existence that essentially concerns the
idea as individual idea.

That individual ideas of the same species not only can but necessarily must, for
all their mutual absolute likeness, separate in existential manifoldness --- that
this existential manifoldness can by no means be put down to the specimens' account
but must be derived from the idea itself --- is a direct consequence of the law of
selfhood: as many principial points of selfhood, so many principles of existence;
so many self-actualizing paradigms, so many individual ideas. If the concretion is
thus to go deeper, it must happen by there forming series of mutually unlike,
essentially different individual ideas, which again presupposes mutually different
species-ideas.

The existential difference between the species-ideas is then at the same time
reflected in an inner difference of essence, a pervasive difference of concept:
such a growing wealth of inner determinations of difference is an essential
presupposition for a rising concretion. Between specimens of \textit{Fragaria
vesca}, for example, there may doubtless show themselves, sensuously regarded, not
a few differences; but these differences concern one and all the outward, the
accidental and vanishing, and do not concern the individual ideas at all; whereas
the difference between a \textit{Fragaria vesca} and a \textit{Fragaria collina},
however much likeness the specimens may have to each other, is a difference
penetrating the individual ideas, a difference which sets the one species-idea
against the other. The fundamental idea out of which the species-ideas develop, and
in which they again reflect their developed content, is the genus-idea. As the
concrete unity of the concrete species-ideas the genus's idea thus now represents
the richer concretion.

Here a concept of genus does not form because different species occur here; but
different species occur here because the genus's idea comes to existence only by
differen%

% --- p. 325 ---
tiating itself into species-ideas, just as the species' idea comes to existence
only by differentiating itself into individual ideas. Far from being more abstract
than the individual ideas, the species-idea is on the contrary more concrete, as
the causal ground in which the existential differences are preformed and after the
development remembered; far from being more abstract than the corresponding
species-ideas, the genus-idea --- in whose causal unity of ground the determinations
of difference developed through the species reflect themselves as moments of
content --- is the more concrete idea.

As little as the species-idea can be an abstraction of individual ideas, so little
can the genus-idea be an abstraction of species-ideas. Not by formal abstraction
but by dialectical reduction does thought reach the general ideas through the
separate ones. The more general an abstracted concept is, the more devoid of
content it must of course be; it goes without saying, then, that formal logic,
which keeps only to abstraction, must let a concept's content stand in inverse
relation to its extension. The more general the idea found by reduction is, the
richer in content it is; for it has all the moments resolved by the reduction in
its content. Dialectic, which goes reduction's way, therefore sets the content of
the idea in a direct and not in an inverse relation to the extension.

The law of concretion decisive for the development of the idea, identical with the
law of selfhood, is already determined when the genus-idea is reached. Instead of
regarding the passage through the individuals to the species, through the species
to the genus, through the genera to the family and so on as a transition from the
more concrete to the more abstract, we must then, according to the law shown,
conversely determine this passage as a deepening into ever richer, ever more inward
concretions.

How the organic specimens through the individual ideas in each species relate to
the species, how the species in each genus essentially relate to the genus, the
genus

% --- p. 326 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
% COLLATION NOTE (pp. 326--328). These three pages exist as a separate edition,
% texts/nielsen/propaedeutik-darwin/translation.tex, made from the British
% Library Google copy and carrying a long contextualising essay. The present
% text is collated against it. Substantive divergences, all deliberate:
%   (1) the excerpt capitalises "Idea" throughout, following Nielsen's Hegelian
%       usage; the book edition has used lower-case "idea" from p. 1 onward and
%       keeps it, for internal consistency;
%   (2) the excerpt renders Bronn's German directly into English; the book
%       edition keeps German verbatim with a bracketed gloss, as everywhere
%       else. The excerpt's English has been reused as that gloss;
%   (3) the excerpt lifts Nielsen's own Danish interpolation out of the middle
%       of Bronn's sentence and sets the German as a display quotation. The
%       book edition follows the Danish as printed, interpolation in place;
%   (4) the excerpt silently normalises the two printer's defects on these
%       pages ("principløs." and "den. Skuende"); both are reproduced here.
through all its species to the family, and so on, can be determined dialectically
by the law of concretion; there is on the other hand no law of development by
which it can be determined \textit{a priori} how many and which species must
necessarily belong to each genus, or how many and which genera originally belong
to each family: philosophical cognition does not go through abstract ideas to
actual facts, but conversely through the actual facts to the concrete ideas.
(Cf.\ Phil. Prop. pp. 158--178).
% (as printed in the Danish: „a priori“ is ANTIQUA against the surrounding
%  Fraktur, and „Phil. Prop.“ is plain Fraktur --- NOT emphasised. The excerpt
%  edition sets „Phil. Prop.“ in italic; that emphasis is the excerpt's, not
%  the book's, and is not carried over.)

If the attempts that have been made in recent times to explain the origin of
species from varieties which in the course of time have become constant are to be
understood as meaning that it is really the circumstances and the external
conditions that determine the organic types, then so unprincipled. and
idea-denying a conception of vital activity must of course meet with
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish, reproduced: a well-formed period stands on
%  the baseline after „principløs“, where the sentence cannot take a stop. The
%  excerpt edition normalises it away.)
contradiction in philosophy. But it ought in this connection not to be forgotten
either that the reality of ideas is infinitely dialectical, and that a dialectical
reality does not let itself be hypostatized in dogmatic positive immediacy. If it
is really so that organic selfhood originally presupposes elementary otherness,
then it follows directly from this that the development of life must also bring
with it a manifold reciprocal action of the typical and the elementary. Varieties,
abortions, displacements and other abnormalities show clearly enough what
influence the elementary conditions must have on the paradigm's
execution\footnote{As regards the investigations belonging here, we refer to
Charles Darwin über die \emph{Entstehung der Arten im Thier-} und Pflanzenreich.
% (as printed in the Danish: „Charles Darwin“ is FRAKTUR, not antiqua, and the
%  German title is Fraktur too, but LETTERSPACED only for the stretch
%  „Entstehung der Arten im Thier-“ --- „über die“ before it and „und
%  Pflanzenreich.“ after the line break are both set close. The emphasis stops
%  at the line break in the middle of the title, a compositor's inconsistency,
%  reproduced as printed.)
Aus dem Englischen übersetzt und mit Anmerkungen versehen von Dr. H. G. Bronn.
Stuttgart 1860. --- ``Es sind'' --- it runs in the ``Schlußwort des Übersetzers'',
pp. 508--9 --- ``die Existenz-Bedingungen, welchen sich die Organismen anpassen
müssen, und welche eine so grosse Rolle in diesem Buche spielen. Sie sind theils
organische und theils unorganische, und die
% --- (the note continues at the foot of p. 327) ---
ersten nach Hrn. Darwin weitaus die zahlreichsten und wichtigsten und daher auch
an und für sich geeignet, die manchfaltigsten Folgen zu veranlassen. Doch eben
diese organischen Prinzipien haben für Darwin wieder den grossen Vortheil, daß,
indem er sich auf ihre Manchfaltigkeit und auf den Kampf ums Daseyn überhaupt
beruft, er der Nothwendigkeit überhoben ist, Rechenschaft von ihrer
Wirkungs-Weise im Einzelnen zu geben und nachzuweisen, welche spezielle Folgen
diese oder jene spezielle organische Bedingung auf die Struktur und Entwickelung
der ihrem Einfluß unterliegenden Organismen überhaupt, oder auf einzelne
insbesondere ausübt. Hr. Darwin beruft sich auf jeder Seite darauf, daß nur solche
Abänderungen Aussicht auf Erhaltung haben, welche dem Individuum und somit der
künftigen Spezies nützlich sind; und theoretisch muß man zugestehen, daß, woferne
es eine natürliche Züchtung gebe, die Sache sich nicht anders verhalten könne.
Aber wir müssen gestehen, doch in fast allen unseren aus angeblich innern Ursachen
hervorgegangenen Varietäten gar nicht finden zu können, worin denn der Nutzen ihrer
Abänderung bestehe\ldots. Warum bekommt z. B. in diesem Kampfe ums Daseyn eine
Pflanzen-Art ovale statt lanzettlicher und die andre lanzettliche statt ovaler
Blätter? warum die eine einen Dolden-artigen und die andre einen Rispen-förmigen
Blüthenstand? warum die eine fünf und die andre vier Staubgefässe, die eine eine
geschlossene und die andre eine weit geöffnete Blüthe? Wozu nützt der einen Dieß
und der andern das Gegentheil? Warum bewirken die organischen Bedingungen Dieß?
Mit welchen Mitteln fangen sie es an? und wie müssen sie beschaffen seyn, um es zu
können? Und wie kann die eine Art der andern dadurch überlegen werden? Wir
gestehen, keinen Zusammenhang zwischen diesen Erscheinungen zu erkennen, und Hr.
Darwin würde uns antworten, daß es möglicher Weise so oder so zugehen
\emph{könne}. Wir gestehen ferner, uns vergeblich um positive Beweise oder auch
nur Belege in dieser Beziehung umgesehen zu haben, manche spezielle Fälle
eigenthümlicher Art etwa ausgenommen; denn wir sind weit entfernt davon, allen
solchen Einfluß überhaupt läugnen zu wollen.'' [These are the conditions of
existence to which organisms must adapt themselves, and which play so large a role
in this book. They are partly organic and partly inorganic, and the former are, in
Mr. Darwin's view, by far the most numerous and important, and for that reason
also in themselves fitted to give rise to the most varied consequences. But these
very organic principles have in turn the great advantage for Darwin that, since he
appeals to their variety and to the struggle for existence in general, he is
relieved of the necessity of accounting for their mode of operation in detail and
of showing what specific consequences this or that specific organic condition
exerts on the structure and development of the organisms subject to its influence,
either in general or individually. Mr. Darwin appeals on every page to the fact
that only those variations have any prospect of survival which are beneficial to
the individual and thus to the future species; and theoretically one must concede
that, if natural selection exists, the matter cannot behave otherwise. But we must
confess that in almost all our varieties said to have arisen from inner causes we
cannot find at all wherein the benefit of their modification consists.... Why does
one plant species acquire, in this struggle for existence, oval instead of
lanceolate leaves, and another lanceolate instead of oval? why does one have an
umbel-like and another a paniculate inflorescence? why does one have five and
another four stamens, one a closed and another a wide-open flower? Of what use to
the one is this, and to the other the opposite? Why do the organic conditions bring
this about? By what means do they set about it, and how must they be constituted to
be capable of it? And how can the one species thereby become superior to the other?
We confess that we can discern no connection between these phenomena, and Mr.
Darwin would answer us that it might possibly happen in one way or another. We
confess further that we have looked in vain for positive proofs or even supporting
evidence in this regard, some special cases of a peculiar kind perhaps excepted;
for we are far from wishing to deny all such influence altogether.]}.

% --- p. 327 ---
% (this page carries only three lines of body text in the Danish; its whole
%  footnote block is the run-over of the *) note anchored on p. 326, given
%  whole at that anchor above. No mark and no counter reset here.)
``The Critique of Pure Reason'' transforms the idea into a subjective
unconditioned; dogmatic positivism hypostatizes the idea into a primordial being
subsisting for itself. Such a positive

% --- p. 328 ---
primordial being is now indeed declared to be an inward, imageless and invisible
being, but becomes precisely, in that the positivity is fixed, by its dogmatic
immediacy an outward, pictorial and intuitable being. The deep beholding, the
immediate beholding of the idea, is in general a mystical-dogmatic beholding of a
negative-positive, an imageless-pictorial; the deep is then recognized especially
by this, that the. beholder does not notice the contradiction.
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish, reproduced: a period stands between „den“
%  and „Skuende“, where the sentence cannot take a stop. The excerpt edition
%  omits it.)
If the immediate beholding of the idea is to acquire scientific significance, that
can happen only by criticism not merely pointing out but also reflecting through
the contradiction breaking forth from all sides. The contradiction is that what is
immediate in nature is so reflected; the contradiction is that what is thus
reflected is so immediate. As little as the exact analysis of infinity is finished
with grasping every finite magnitude as an integral of infinitely small magnitudes,
just as little is the dialectical analysis of the idea concluded with the immediate
reflecting itself in the reflected; for the articulating intermediate determinations
which develop from this are innumerable.

The kingdom of ideas is, in relation to the outer plant world grounded therein, a
system of reflection-existences, content-rich remembrances of existence; the ideas'
reflection in the reflected immediacy of the individual forms is, like the light's
clarity, a transfigured immediacy. In this clarity the specimen-existences are seen
through their individual ideas reflected in the species' idea, the species-ideas
with their individual ideas reflected in the genus's idea, the genus-ideas with
their species-ideas in the family's, through the family's in the order's, through
the order's in the class's, through the class's in the series', through the series'
concrete, content-rich idea in the kingdom's fundamental idea, in the depth.

\subsection*{β) Animal Life.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{β) Animal Life}

As organic nature the idea is life, and living existence, essentially regarded, an
idea-existence; but idea-existence

% --- p. 329 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
presupposes a totalized opposition of the inner and the outer, and this opposition
again presupposes its totalized unity. In that selfhood gains in subjective
subsistence by concentrating itself into a self that senses itself, a soul, and
thus makes the ideal independent, the corresponding organism at the same time gains
objective subsistence in a closed system of organs and limbs.

As orienting points of view we choose: a juxtaposition of animal and plant; a
consideration of animal individuality, so far as its fundamental features let
themselves be determined by the general relation between animals and animal series;
and finally a survey of the manifold of typical-teleological characters in the
animal world.

\emph{αα) Animal and plant.} To state the categorial difference between plant and
animal is by no means the same as to enumerate the marks by which one would in
every case be in a position to decide whether a given living thing is to be
reckoned to the plant or to the animal kingdom. The latter task is of practical
interest and has, as regards the lowest forms, as is well known, its great
difficulties\footnote{The \textit{Æthalium septicum}, known among tanners under the
name of ``troll's butter'', which Fries reckoned to the \textit{Myxogasteres}
(slime fungi), De Bary (Zeitschrift für wissenschaftl. Zool. von Siebold und
Kölliker, Vol. X. 1860. pp. 89--168) has now reckoned to the animals
(\textit{Mycetozoa}). Out of the spore there comes forth, namely, a being
resembling the infusorian \textit{Euglena}, which moves freely about by means of
one or two swimming-hairs and which has one or two pulsating vacuoles. After two or
three days the swimming-hair is lost; the body becomes more round, the movement
slower, and now the individual looks like an \textit{Amoeba}. There now forms a
branched slime-mass which flows about in the moist bark; many of these small
branched slime-threads unite and form a net, and so on. Have we here now an animal
or a plant? From the movements it cannot be seen, for the algae's swarm-spores have
movement too; from the inner structure it cannot be
% --- (the note continues at the foot of p. 330 in the Danish) ---
seen either; for there is indeed here no trace of nerves, but in amoebae --- in so
far as one takes them for animals --- and in rhizopods one discovers no trace of
nerves either. The argument that an organism which takes up solid matter as
nourishment must be an animal is, in a case like the present one, where the process
of nutrition is so obscure, of no significance. Against the analogy with amoebae
and rhizopods, as regards the mode of development and the so-called sarcode slime,
there stands an equally predominant analogy with the fungi.};

% --- p. 330 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
the former, on the other hand, is of purely theoretical nature and offers no
difficulty whatever. The animal has a soul, the plant not; this distinction is as
much a matter of principle as it is popular.

So then: the animal is ensouled; but what really is a soul? If we seek what is
peculiar to the soul in this, that in its immediate existing it is a sensing
being\footnote{That the investigations by which it is to be decided whether an
organic phenomenon is to be reckoned under the plant or under the animal kingdom
must at last, when the circle of observations is closed, rest on categorial
determinations, and so far on a fixing of concepts, goes without saying. --- ``Ich
mache ferner darauf aufmerksam, daß die neueren Untersuchungen der niederen
Organismen und alle Grenzstreitigkeiten zwischen Thier- und Pflanzenreich zwar
einerseits zur Einsicht in eine ausserordentlich mannigfache und innige Berührung
beider Reiche, aber andererseits in gleichem Grade zu einer immer schärferen
Auffassung der Grenzlinie und zur bestimmteren Darstellung der diesen Reichen zu
Grunde liegenden Begriffe geführt haben, mögen auch von manchen Forschern im
Widerspruch mit ihren positiven Leistungen Zweifel an dieser theoretischen Wahrheit
in wenig klarer Weise erhoben worden sein.'' [I draw attention further to this,
that the more recent investigations of the lower organisms and all the boundary
disputes between the animal and the plant kingdom have on the one hand led to
insight into an extraordinarily manifold and intimate contact of the two kingdoms,
but on the other hand in equal degree to an ever sharper apprehension of the
boundary line and to a more determinate presentation of the concepts lying at the
ground of these kingdoms --- however much doubt about this theoretical truth may
also have been raised in a not very clear manner by some investigators, in
contradiction with their own positive achievements.] A. Wigand. Ueber die Stellung
der Myxomycetes zu dem Thier- und Pflanzenreich. Jahrbücher für wissenschaftl.
Botanik. herausg. von N. Pringsheim. Vol. III. Berlin 1861. p. 48.}, then there
follows from this a threefold task: to determine what sensation is, how sensation
relates to arbitrariness, and how the functions of sensation and of arbitrariness

% --- p. 331 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
relate to the organs, the material media, by which they are conditioned.

To make clear what lies in the concept of sensation, we must above all make a sharp
distinction between \emph{sensation and physical affection.} So long as the
reciprocal action of natural things is grasped chiefly in a witty subjective way,
so that consciousness is guided by the immediate total impression, it is especially
the mysterious in the phenomena that easily becomes an intimation of a mystical
inward, an utterance of the soul dwelling in things. The magnetic needle's
restlessness intimates that it has a sensation of a piece of iron moving in its
neighbourhood; the rubbed amber which draws the thread to itself is as though
ensouled\footnote{\textit{Genera ejus plura.} --- says Pliny
(\textit{hist. natur. XXXVII. 12.}) of amber --- \textit{Attritu digitorum accepta
caloris anima trahunt in se paleas}\ldots\ [Its kinds are several. --- Having
received a breath of warmth by the rubbing of the fingers, they draw chaff to
themselves.] A. Humboldt. Kosmos, Danish translation. Part I. p. 158. Note
(p. 105). Cf.\ Aristotel. \textit{De anima I. 2.}}. When in the chemical process
the substances are separated and united according to elective affinity, it looks
for all the world as though it were after all love (φιλία) and strife (νεῖκος),
sympathy and antipathy --- thus real sensations, inclinations and moods --- from
which the elements' reciprocal action essentially proceeded\footnote{Aber
Geselligkeit --- says M. J. Schleiden jestingly --- ist, wie beim Menschen, so bei
den Elementen die erste Stufe der Gesittung; die verwandtschaftlichen Beziehungen
unter den Elementen, wie die Chemie es sinnig nennt, führen zur Paarung. Die
schmutzige Kohle wird in ihrer Verbindung mit dem reinen Sauerstoff zur
appetitlichen Kohlensäure, die im Champagner aufbraust; der scharfe und feindselige
Sauerstoff mildert sich in seiner Ehe mit dem flüchtigen zarten Wasserstoff zum
weich sich anschmiegenden Wasser; dieselbe luftige Fee weiß den grämlichen und
ungeselligen Stickstoff zu fesseln, und siehe da, das flüchtig reizende
Ammoniakgas ist das Kind dieser Verbindung. Oder sage ich lieber so: Kohlensäure,
Wassergas und Ammoniak sind die Seelen des rohen Elementarlebens; fertig gebildet
schwingen
% --- (the note continues at the foot of p. 332 in the Danish) ---
sie sich zu einem höheren Dasein auf; sie gehen über in die Pflanze, in welcher für
sie ein neuer Läuterungsproceß beginnt. [But sociability --- as with human beings,
so with the elements --- is the first stage of civilization; the relations of
kinship among the elements, as chemistry ingeniously calls them, lead to pairing.
The dirty coal becomes, in its combination with pure oxygen, the appetizing carbonic
acid that effervesces in champagne; the sharp and hostile oxygen is softened, in its
marriage with the volatile delicate hydrogen, into water that nestles softly against
one; the same airy fairy knows how to fetter the morose and unsociable nitrogen, and
behold, the volatile stimulating ammonia gas is the child of this union. Or shall I
rather put it thus: carbonic acid, water vapour and ammonia are the souls of the raw
elementary life; fully formed, they swing themselves up to a higher existence; they
pass over into the plant, in which a new process of purification begins for them.]
Die Beseelung der Pflanzen. Studien. Leipzig 1855. pp. 149--150.}. The glass that
rings

% --- p. 332 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
lets its voice be heard, and makes as it were an attempt to speak. If the affection
is a sensation, why should the sound not be a voice? ``Everything that exists has a
voice, has an utterance, but they are accordingly. The rod that strikes on a bell
also gets a sensation of the stroke; it quivers and trembles to its innermost, but
it can get nothing out of it; it shakes and quivers, but no one knows of it --- at
most the hand that holds it. The bell, on the other hand, is a nobler being; it
works up the impression and answers with a high, clear, penetrating tone. With it
the impression becomes an outburst''\footnote{N. M. Petersen. Forelæsn. vedkmd. de
nordiske Sprog. Indbydelsesskr. til Kbhvns Universit. Fest. 1861. p. 5.}.

``The whole of nature'' --- says Perty\footnote{Perty. Allgem. Naturgesch. Vol. I.
p. 110.} --- ``is in the strictest sense living and ensouled. All changes in the
single things of nature, in so far as they are not mechanical, are a work of the
forces or souls dwelling in them or seizing them from without.'' --- But when
