elementary forces are identified with soul-forces, when everything in nature lives
and every atom has its own soul (for Perty assumes this too, that every atom is a
soul\footnote{Jedes Atom ist ein für sich selbstständig Bestehendes, von einer Seele
Bewegtes --- ist nur diese Seele niederster Art selbst. [Every atom is something
subsisting independently for itself, moved by a soul --- only that this soul is
itself of the lowest kind.] Work cited. p. 115.}), then it can of course be subject
to no doubt either that the plant too must have a soul. ``We assume, then, that
there is a soul in the plant too. The plant also has an activity that goes out
beyond the present. It pursues a distant end, and that not merely passively,
automatically, roughly as when the movements in the

% --- p. 333 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
wound clockwork complete their circuit, but it strives self-actively, with a certain
freedom in the choice of its means, with a persistent, as it were deliberate zeal
toward this end. It seeks to preserve itself, it sets itself in opposition to the
surrounding world, it combats and conquers the material lying outside it in order to
appropriate it. It grows and multiplies by this appropriation, which it does not
undertake and carry out blindly but with a certain choice and a certain care. Under
this constant, continually renewed conflict it mediates for itself not merely a
material increase of body, but it provides for something which does not yet exist,
for something like itself which is first to come, for an offspring. It prepares this
last result of its many-sidedly living activity with great independence, consistency
and purposiveness, forms it out with great perfection in its functions, and thus
links, with quiet mysterious carefulness, the present to a distant
future\ldots. We therefore do the plant a wrong, we misjudge its nature and dignity,
when we regard it as though it were not, just as well as the animal, endowed with a
primal force leading to determinate activities --- a force not determining from
without, but bearing all the conditions of existence within itself and completing
its organic unity. Yet I believe I have by these words sufficiently intimated that
the quiet plant world too, seemingly so closed off and as it were sunk into itself,
has a soul''\footnote{K. Ph. v. Martius. Die Seele der Pflanzen. Reden und Vorträge
aus dem Gebiete der Naturforschung. Stuttgart und Tübing. 1838. pp. 271--74.}.

What is ambiguous, unclear and untenable in this way of considering things is easy
to discover. Here one must distinguish, and distinguish sharply, between the
lifeless and the living, between the merely living and the ensouled, between
physical affection and psychical

% --- p. 334 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
sensation\footnote{In an address as acute as it is charming and witty --- Studien.
Die Beseel. d. Pfl. --- Schleiden has taken to task those physiologists who in
their mystical obscurity still continue to endow the plant with a soul. --- ``Du
weißt, wenn ich auch mit diesen Herren böse thue, daß das Ganze doch Nichts ist,
als eine Variation auf das alte Shakespeare'sche Thema: \emph{„Ihr guten Leute,
aber schlechten Musikanten.“} Wie in aller Welt will man es denn rechtfertigen,
daß ein Mann deshalb, weil er ein guter Botaniker oder Physiker ist, auch
nothwendig ein guter Philosoph sein müsse\ldots. Doch ich verirre mich schon
wieder. Ich wollte von \emph{Martius und Fechner} sprechen, die Beide gute Leute,
aber schlechte Musikanten --- verzeihe, vielmehr Philosophen --- sind. Martius mit
seiner Seele und seiner Unsterblichkeit der Pflanzen macht mir wenig Noth. Beides
sind so oberflächliche und verwaschene Träume, daß Nichts daran zu widerlegen ist,
weil man Nichts packen kann; es sind wesenlose Nebelbilder\ldots. Bedeutender als
Gegner scheint Fechner zu sein, schon deshalb, weil wir es bei ihm nicht mit einem
flüchtig gefaßten und flüchtig hingeworfenen Gedanken, sondern mit einem dicken
Buche --- the reference is to: Nanna oder über das Seelenleben der Pflanze von
G. Th. Fechner, Leipzig 1848 ---, mit zahlreichen Citaten und anderm gelehrten
Flitterstaat zu thun haben. Aber nur um so trauriger! Ein Stoff für ein reizendes
kleines Verschen so breit getreten, daß er wissenschaftlich wie ästhetisch
widerlich wird\ldots. Bei Gelegenheit der Frage, ob die Pflanze Nerven besitze,
sagt er: „Mir scheint der Faserstoff der Pflanzen, wenn man doch einmal Fasern
verlangt, ebenso gut geeignet und für die Pflanzen passender zu Nerven, als das
thierische Eiweiß.“ Der einzige Satz zeigt die absoluteste Unwissenheit in Bezug
auf die innere Organisation der Pflanze. Faserstoff, in der Bedeutung, wie es alle
Naturforscher ohne Ausnahme jetzt anwenden, kommt organisirt in keiner Pflanze vor;
Pflanzenfaser, von der man wohl noch vor funfzig Jahren zuweilen sprach, ist ein
Ding, das nicht existirt; Fasern als selbstständige Organisationselemente giebt es
in der ganzen Pflanze nicht. [You know that even when I deal harshly with these
gentlemen, the whole thing is nothing but a variation on the old Shakespearean
theme: good people, but poor musicians. How in all the world is one to justify that
a man, because he is a good botanist or physicist, must necessarily also be a good
philosopher?... But I am straying again. I wanted to speak of Martius and Fechner,
who are both good people but poor musicians --- forgive me, rather philosophers.
Martius with his soul and his immortality of plants gives me little trouble. Both
are such superficial and washed-out dreams that there is nothing in them to refute,
because one can get hold of nothing; they are insubstantial cloud-pictures.... More
considerable as an opponent Fechner seems to be, if only because with him we have to
do not with a hastily conceived and hastily thrown-off thought but with a thick
book, with numerous citations and other learned tinsel. But so much the sadder!
Material for a charming little verse spread so thin that it becomes scientifically
as well as aesthetically repellent.... On the occasion of the question whether the
plant possesses nerves, he says: the fibre-substance of plants seems to me, if
fibres are to be demanded at all, just as well suited and more fitting for plants as
nerves than animal albumen. This single sentence shows the most absolute ignorance
regarding the inner organization of the plant. Fibre-substance, in the sense in
which all investigators of nature without exception now employ it, occurs organized
in no plant; plant-fibre, of which one may still have spoken now and then fifty
years ago, is a thing that does not exist; fibres as independent elements of
organization do not exist in the whole plant.] Work cited. pp. 153--155.}.
% (QUOTE DEFECT, as printed in the Danish: the long Schleiden quotation of this
%  note opens „Du weißt, wenn ich auch … and is NEVER closed --- the note simply
%  ends „Anfr. Skr. S. 153--155.“ with no closing mark. Mirrored here, leaving
%  one unmatched opener in this span.)

But if it is a blunder to want to vindicate ideality's right in so immediate a way
that the idea is without more ado made one with life, and life with the soul:

% --- p. 335 ---
then it is on the other side a no less dangerous blunder to let the understanding
lay so decisive a critical emphasis on the separation of the ideal and the
material, the living and the lifeless, the ensouled and the unensouled, that the
difference lacks identity and the whole conception becomes without idea. Once the
conception has become without idea, then only one of two ways out remains: either
critical understanding must halt at the antinomy between nature and spirit as at an
insurmountable barrier, or it must, as by a decree of power, proclaim naturalness's
absolute sovereignty and reduce the spiritual to a physical utterance of the
material. Which way out the consciousness caught in the critique of the
understanding is to choose then by no means comes to rest on scientific insight;
no, the choice is here decided in another way, namely by promptings. Some, that is,
have their promptings from freedom --- ``die unbedingte Freiheit''! Others from
necessity --- ``die absolute Nothwendigkeit''! Which of these promptings is to
become the ruling one in a given subjectivity is chiefly conditioned by the mood,
and so comes to depend on whether the mood ruling in the mind, the fundamental
mood, is --- to use an expression of mood answering to the dilemma --- either more
ideal or more bestial.

The first thing peculiar to the soul as soul, the mark by which the soul-principle
distinguishes itself precisely from the general principle of life, is sensation.
But what is sensation? Immediate self-reflection, the self's unconscious
self-discovery, its impression of itself under the impression of another, a finding
of itself in which it finds itself placed. However one defines the expression, it
will always show itself that sensation concerns the existing self: where the self
is not reflected as self there is no sensation. As immediate self-reflection,
sensation is at the same time consciousness's most immediate utterance. Since
sensation is thus what is most immediate in con%

% --- p. 336 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
sciousness, only the sensing being can notice what sensation is; it lies in the
nature of the case that there is here nothing more comprehensible with which one
could begin. But just as on the whole nothing immediate lets itself be determined
except by reflection, so sensation cannot be grasped, held fast and characterized
as sensation either, without a reflecting consciousness. Nothing less than a human
self-consciousness --- and indeed a consciousness familiar with the dialectic of
the categories --- is required in order to determine this unreflected reflection
and make clear the category of sensation.

If a dialectician were to begin without more ado with the \emph{proposition: there
are sensations which are not sensed, and these are precisely the original
fundamental sensations,} then empirical-critical understanding would easily feel
% (the gesperrt run in the Danish begins at „Sætning:“ --- the word itself is
%  spaced, unlike „Dersom en Dialektiker“ in the line above --- and ends at
%  „Grundfornemmelser,“.)
repelled and regard the setting up of such a contradiction as proof of a one-sided
bent for the paradoxical. But what are we now to say when the physiologist --- the
empirically investigating, critically reflecting physiologist himself --- directly
avows it as a main proposition, a fundamental proposition decisive for physiology,
that consciousness begins with sensations which are not noticed at all?

``Consciousness in the wider sense'' --- says Lewes\footnote{Hverdagsl. Physiol. 2.
p. 71.} --- ``is the sum of all our sensations, the union of many streams of
sensibility. Consciousness in the narrower sense is only another expression for
sensibility: we have as many different forms of sensibility as we have different
kinds of sensations. While some of these confluent streams are so weak that they
are noticed as little as the stars at midday, others are so quiet and steady that
they attract no attention unless their course is disturbed. Thus healthy people are
scarcely able to distinguish the sensations called forth by the circulation of the
blood or by the intes%

% --- p. 337 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
tines' movement; but an insignificant change in these sensations is felt at once.''

How dialectical the category of sensation must be can already be seen here from the
consequences to which an acute reflection --- guided by observations, but by no
means attentive to the dialectical --- is led with inner necessity.

``Let us see'' --- it runs in an earlier place\footnote{Work cited 2.
pp. 49--50.} --- ``whether greater exactness in language will not help us in our
investigation. It is maintained on all sides that sensations cannot take place
without consciousness. Let that be so; but when one comes to speak of consciousness
itself, we find that the brain is regarded as its only seat, and from this one
concludes of course that no sensation can be produced by an impression unless that
impression reaches the brain. When it turns out that an animal without a brain
gives all the signs of feeling that it gave when it had its brain, the
physiologists say that one must not believe that the brainless animal really has
sensations; far from it! it has \emph{only sensitive impressions which call forth
reflex movements} without any consciousness in the animal. To have sensations and
to be conscious of sensations is one and the same. To have a sensation and to know
that one has it are two different things, not the same. Knowledge cannot be found
without consciousness; but consciousness can be found --- and often is found ---
without knowledge.''

Is the riddle now solved by this? By no means! One may indeed try to veil the
contradiction by laying weight on a remark such as this, that the physiologist here
does not speak directly of sensations which are not sensed\footnote{In order to
avoid the ``glaring contradictions'' (Work cited 2. p. 51) Lewes will define
sensation as ``the mere reaction of the sensitive organism'' (p. 55); but precisely
on this occasion it
% --- (the note continues at the foot of p. 338 in the Danish) ---
shows how little mere explanations of words, definitions and distinctions avail
when the matter itself is dialectical. The suppressed contradiction bobs up every
moment and steals up behind the author, Thus one does well on the
% (as printed in the Danish: „Forfatteren, Saaledes“ --- a comma where the
%  sense requires a full stop, and the following word capitalised. The comma
%  is reproduced at the same place in the English sentence.)
one side (p. 54) ``to hold fast the fundamental proposition that to have a
sensation and to be conscious of it is one and the same;'' but on the other side
(p. 56) one does well again in not holding this fundamental proposition fast; for
the question ``Does the sensation cease during the time one ceases to be conscious
of it?'' is expressly answered with: ``No, the impression has continued.'' The
sensation thus does not cease because one ceases to have the sensation; one can
thus have sensations which one does not have.}, but of

% --- p. 338 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
sensations which are not noticed, not expressly brought to consciousness; but what
does one attain by this? To lay the contradiction into consciousness itself! One is
by the consequence forced to concede that there is an unconscious consciousness,
and then we are no better off. ``The fact that we have a sensitive organism is
indisputable, as is that this organism is incessantly excited by outer and inner
stimuli. Every such excitation of the sensitive organism must be a sensation. These
sensations must necessarily become very different according as the excited organs
and the exciting causes are different; but they must all be sensations; they are
all active states of the general property sensibility. They must therefore all be
moments in consciousness\footnote{Work cited 2. pp. 50--51.}.''

Before we attempt, however, a closer clarification of consciousness's unconscious
moments --- sensations which are not sensed --- we shall first consider quite
generally the fundamental relation between \emph{sensation and arbitrariness.} The
selfhood that senses itself is indeed passive under the affection, but is
% (this gesperrt run straddles a line break in the Danish („Vilkaar-/lighed.“)
%  and spacing.py does NOT report it; found by eye at 600 dpi, exactly parallel
%  to „Fornemmelse og physisk Affection.“ on p. 331.)
nevertheless by this passivity determined

% --- p. 339 ---
to immediate self-determination; from this arbitrariness springs. In
arbitrariness's springing forth the same contradiction shows itself as in
sensation's. Just as on the passive side one begins with sensations which are not
sensed, so on the active side one begins with utterances of arbitrariness which are
not arbitrary. Arbitrariness presupposes a choice, a choice presupposes
deliberation, deliberation again reflection; but in the self's first dark,
immediate utterance of arbitrariness there is as little freedom of choice as there
is reflection in the first dark, immediate self-sensation. And yet there is here in
this first darkness both reflection and freedom of choice: the involuntary
arbitrariness bears within it the disposition to freedom of choice, just as the
self-sensation which does not sense itself bears within it the disposition to
reflection. How naturally the unreflected and reflection, the involuntary and
arbitrariness, go together in a unity of soul is seen for example when an animal is
suddenly seized by fear and flees.

Although arbitrariness can in many ways make itself known in outward-going
movements, and although its utterances in manifold cases bear such distinct traces
of freedom of choice that there can be no doubt that what so moves must be
something ensouled --- still, self-determination is and remains always something
inward, and as something inward cannot possibly be known and determined by an
observation which must keep to the merely outward. A movement can outwardly look
like an utterance of arbitrariness and yet be due to a physical necessity;
conversely, a manifestation of life can look as though it proceeded from physical
necessity and yet stem from an arbitrariness, in so far as it is conditioned by
self-sensation, is a reaction of a self that senses itself. That it must in this
respect be extremely difficult for the investigator of nature to distinguish
between the plant and the lower animal is under%

% --- p. 340 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
standable\footnote{\ldots. es fragt sich\ldots. noch, ob und in wiefern ein Maß
der Empfindung selbst und des Geistigen überhaupt möglich sei. Factisch ist bis
jetzt kein solches vorhanden oder, vorsichtiger gesprochen, bis jetzt als solches
anerkannt, vielmehr bis auf die neueste Zeit bezweifelt oder geleugnet worden, daß
ein solches überhaupt zu finden. Selbst Herbarts Versuch einer mathematischen
Psychologie hat nicht auf einem solchen zu fussen vermocht; der wichtigste
Einwand, den man ihm von jeher entgegengehalten hat; ungeachtet Herbart das Maß so
zu sagen in den Händen hatte. [.... It is further a question .... whether and how
far a measure of sensation itself, and of the spiritual in general, is possible.
In fact none such is at present available or, to speak more cautiously, none is at
present acknowledged as such; rather it has until the most recent times been
doubted or denied that such a thing is to be found at all. Even Herbart's attempt
at a mathematical psychology has not been able to rest on one --- the weightiest
objection that has always been brought against him, notwithstanding that Herbart
had the measure, so to speak, in his hands.] G. Th. Fechner. Psychophysik. 1.
pp. 54--55.},
% (as printed in the Danish: „hat; ungeachtet“ --- a semicolon where the sense
%  requires a comma or a full stop.)
just as it is also understandable that those physiologists who still dream of plant
souls will easily find telling phenomena --- phenomena which, especially for the
consideration full of intimations, point toward a secret longing, a quiet
sensation, a dark arbitrariness.

When the scientific botanist has carefully investigated the phenomena of movement
in the plant kingdom, the total impression will --- even if not all phenomena let
themselves be sufficiently explained --- confirm the assumption of a difference in
principle between plant and animal, a difference which on the whole shines forth
from the peculiarity of the opposed organisms. --- ``Even if in a great many cases
we still cannot explain the first cause of the movements in the plant kingdom, yet
at least the mechanism of the organs of movement is pretty well known, just as the
phenomena themselves let themselves for the most part be grasped as phenomena of
tension --- either in consequence of a contraction of the parts which sets in at
different times during desiccation, or in consequence of local turgescence and
slackening in determinate parenchymatous tissues, phenomena which are probably due
to a diffusion and stand under the influence of the unity of life in the whole
plant. Muscles and nerves, which in the higher animal classes condition the
movements, are lacking in plants; the phenomena of movement in the higher groups of
the plant kingdom cannot for that reason well be compared with the

% --- p. 341 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
arbitrary movements in the animal kingdom, which stand under the sensorium's
influence\footnote{H. Schacht. Lehrbuch der Anatomie und Physiologie der Gewächse.
Part II. Berlin 1859. pp. 504--505.}.''

If ``the living body'' is set quite generally against the non-living, the organic
against the inorganic, the fundamental likeness between plant and animal is
doubtless unmistakable; but if anyone now on that ground assumes that the
difference between plant and animal must be less pervasive, he is caught in a great
error. From the elementary to the organic the transition can happen only by a
corresponding principle; from plant life to animal life the transition can likewise
happen only by a corresponding principle. But just as the plant is raised above the
elementary only by assimilating the elementary to itself and organizing the
inorganic, so is the animal again raised above the plant only by assimilating the
organic to itself\footnote{It is by no means overlooked here that plants also need
organic substances, and that conversely animals nourish themselves with inorganic
substances which they take directly from the earth, the air and the water; what is
thought of here is rather the transformation which the whole vegetative life
undergoes in the animal organism.} and transforming the whole system of vegetative
functions into a foundation for the animal.

Outwardly regarded, animal life may doubtless be said to run through two quite
different stages: a first and lower one, where in its embryonal condition it is
still only a plant life; a later and higher one, where the utterances of sensation
and arbitrariness intimate that the hitherto merely vegetating organism has
acquired a soul. But these two stages cannot possibly be thought separated in such
a way that the animal organism should really begin with a life without soul and
thereafter, on receiving a soul into the bargain, so to speak, live both

% --- p. 342 ---
with life and with soul. How absurd such an assumption is shines forth as much from
the nature of the case as from the physiological phenomena.

If the animal principle of life were not originally a soul-principle, the origin of
sensation and of arbitrariness would be an absolutely insoluble riddle. A sensation
which is not yet sensed cannot possibly have reality unless it has its ideal
reality in a corresponding principle of sensation. The principle of sensation,
which from the beginning is not itself sensing, sets out precisely to procure
sensation's physical conditions, to articulate its organism in such a way that
self-sensation's end can be attained once the organs, the requisite means and
media, have been procured. That the end is attained is then marked by the
organizing principle expressly taking possession of its organism and thus uttering
itself as arbitrariness's ideal centre, as the self that senses itself. Since now
self-sensation is dialectically one with arbitrariness, the principle of sensation
must \textit{eo ipso} be principle of arbitrariness: in the identity of the
principle of sensation and the principle of arbitrariness we recognize the
soul-principle.

If now the animal's life is essentially one with the soul, then it will also be
seen from this that the unity of life is by no means weakened by the greater
manifold of organs and manifestations of life which distinguish the higher
individuals over the lower. On the contrary! the more articulated the organism in
question is, the more centralized is life again in the whole: the greater the
manifold, the stronger the unity; the richer the means, the more decisive the
purpose; the more numerous the heterogeneous moments of selfhood, the more powerful
the selfhood.

The functions of conscious life are seen from this point of view in a twofold
relation to the organs in question, namely: \emph{1) in a material relation of
dependence,} in so far as they are point for point conditioned by the organisms'
physical con%
% --- p. 343 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
stitution; \emph{2) in an ideal relation of independence,} in so far as the
functions, by assuming reflection's form, at the same time show themselves as
separate utterances of the principle which has formed the organs and thus itself
determined the conditions on which its functions rest. \emph{The reciprocal action
of the functions of consciousness with their organs}, and by way of the organs with
one another, is then according to this doubling of relations determined not merely
by the organs' bodily \emph{unity of connection}, but in the last instance by the
organic unity of principle --- the psychical \emph{unity of self}, articulated in
and by the whole body.

The cognition of the unity of life ruling in ensouled organisms has in recent times
been far too unduly forced back by the preponderant attention which physiology ---
doubtless in accordance with the natural course of research --- has had to direct
to the life of the parts, especially to ``cell life''. ``The last twenty years'',
says Lewes\footnote{Work cited 2. pp. 439--40.}, ``will go down in history under
the name of the cell period, so manifold have the investigations been and so
striking the results as regards the role the cells play .... The cell is regarded
as the true biological atom. Nothing lives but cells, or what can be directly
traced back to cells ... Every cell in the organism is independent: it is born, it
grows, it reproduces itself, and it dies, as if it were a single-celled plant or a
single-celled animal. An organ's growth and decay is like a nation's or a tree's
growth and decay. The single cells of which the organ is composed grow and die,
just as the single human beings or the single leaves grow and die. There is a
certain aggregate unity, but it consists of separate individuals. Just as a
nation's or a tree's life is the sum of the life in all their single parts, so the
life in an organism is the sum of the life in its single cells. This is one of the
great revelations of the newer science. One no longer speaks

% --- p. 344 ---
of a mid-point for vital activity; for one knows that there are myriads of
mid-points.''

The logical half-heartedness by which this continually returning doctrine of
aggregation distinguishes itself has in our foregoing already been illuminated in
many places and from many sides. It is not enough that the conceptual relation
between the whole and its parts, the inner and the outer, is not attended to at
all, and that there can thus be no thought of anything resembling an attempt at a
scientific presentation of the concept: organism; but even the images, analogies
and similes which are here offered us in the concepts' place are so carelessly
applied that even a fleeting glance must see their untenability. The life in the
single cell is here compared with the single person's life in the nation. But, we
must ask, does it really hold of the single person in the nation that he is so
wholly and entirely independent that he is born, grows, develops and dies as if he
alone made up the whole nation, the whole state? Now if this yields no good sense,
how can it then yield a good sense that the single cell in the organism should be so
wholly and entirely independent that it can be born, can grow, can reproduce itself
and die, as if it were an organism for itself, an isolated organism, a single-celled
plant or a single-celled animal? Instead of a repetition of the general we shall,
in order if possible to discover the mid-point ruling in the many ``mid-points'',
prefer in this connection an investigation of an organism peculiar in its kind. Here
is, for example, a fertilized bird's egg --- more closely, a hen's egg. That there
are here in the yolk's myriads of cells myriads of relative mid-points may in a way
be conceded; but when the question is whether in this manifold of mid-points there
should not show itself an originally ruling mid-point, then we need only refer to
the germ. ``It is the germ that little by little grows and transforms itself into a

% --- p. 345 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
chicken, while the yolk and the white are at bottom only the \emph{nutritive
material} at whose cost this growth and transformation of the germ is brought
about.'' (Eschricht\footnote{Tolv Foredr. p. 3.}).

Such as this whole disposition still is in its first stage, one may indeed from one
point of view want to see in the germ and the yolk an aggregate of cells; but this
aggregate is nevertheless no aggregate. In an aggregate it must expressly be
presupposed that the single parts have an origin independent of the whole and of
one another; the aggregate comes about precisely by constituents already at hand
being heaped together and connected in an outward way. Now are the cells of the
germ and the yolk in this way each for itself independent, so that with respect to
origin and individual subsistence they maintain themselves in independence both of
the whole and of one another? One does not need to be any great physiologist to see
how false, how altogether untenable and fundamentally false such a mode of
conception is. In the great swarm of yolk cells which come forth by ``cleavage''
there is not a single one of which it can with truth be said that it is by its
origin independent, that it is born, that it grows, that it reproduces itself and
dies, as if it were an isolated organism, a single-celled plant or a single-celled
animal.

That every cell at every point is a part for itself only by being a moment for the
whole, that its peculiar disposition is preformed in and with the whole's
disposition, its single life in and with the development of life in the whole, is
then seen clearly enough in the beholding of the series of planned changes,
answering to the paradigm's demands, which the germ undergoes. ``The changes'' ---
it runs --- ``which take place in the germ on the first day are the following: it
spreads out somewhat more to all sides and separates into two layers, of which the
upper is

% --- p. 346 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
more transparent and is called the \emph{animal} or \emph{animalic} layer, the
lower is more milk-white and is called the \emph{vegetative} or \emph{mucous
layer.} That they are so called is because out of the upper there develop ... all
the organs for the manifestations of life proper to animals: the nervous system,
the organs of sense and of movement; out of the lower, on the other hand, the
organs for the nutritive life, namely the intestinal tube, the lungs and all the
glandular ducts. Thus the division of the body's different organs into those which
represent the animal and those which represent the vegetative life is shown us by
nature itself in the germ's first the forming. Soon, however, a third layer forms
between these two. It is called the \emph{vascular layer}, for in it there develop
the heart, the blood vessels and the blood itself --- thus as it were a system
common to both those classes of organs.'' (Eschricht\footnote{Tolv Foredr.
pp. 4--5. --- ``According to Bischof there is this peculiarity in the human egg,
that these layers lie in the reverse order --- the animal layer inward, the
vegetative, which is at the same time the intestine's first disposition, outward.
Reichert and Remak assume a different division of the germinal membrane.''
Physiol. af Valentin ved Hannover. p. 729.}).
% (as printed in the Danish: „i Kimens første Formen“, with a doubled
%  definite --- the compositor's or Eschricht's, not repaired. Rendered here
%  as „the germ's first the forming“.
%  Letterspacing on this page skips the small connective: the print sets
%  „animale“ and „dyriske“ gesperrt but NOT the „eller“ between them, and NOT
%  the following „Blad,“; likewise „vegetative“ … „Sliimbladet.“)

Already the phenomena of formation which incubation on the first day thus brings
about can, when their essence is thought through, sufficiently establish the undue
superficiality of the doctrine of aggregation. If we keep to the interpretation
given --- and another interpretation can in any case only alter, by no means cancel
the fundamental thought --- then it is after all altogether incontestable that the
organization which takes place in the animal layer must be essentially different
from that which takes place in the vegetative. The organization peculiar to each
layer presupposes a peculiar formation of cells. Every cell's transient single life
is inwardly determined by a corresponding energy of remembrance, a total effect of
the peculi%
% --- p. 347 ---
arity of the layer to which it belongs; conversely the layer's peculiarity is again
determined by the peculiar cells. The dialectic of organizing --- that all the
single cells and cell-points in each layer derive their inner peculiarity from the
formative drive ruling in the layer, while the layer itself again obtains its
peculiarity from the formative activity punctualized in the swarm of cells --- is
precisely a categorial expression for the unity ruling in the manifold. As little
as any single cell can here lead a life independent of its corresponding layer,
just as little can the formations in the one layer at any point whatever take place
independently of the formations in the other. Here there does not form an animal
layer alongside a vegetative one, as if the one of these formations were originally
no concern of the other; no, here an \emph{animal} layer forms \emph{because} a
\emph{vegetative} one forms here; here \emph{such} an animal one forms
\emph{because} \emph{such} a vegetative one forms here: the one of these formations
is with all its peculiarity essentially grounded in the other. Nor is it then on
account of outward circumstances, nor in consequence of a heaping together of a
quantity of mutually independent cells, that the vascular layer comes in and
accidentally yields an outer connecting link between the two others; no, the
vascular layer's formation is --- in an earlier connection we have already
illuminated what preformation means --- originally preformed in that unity of
disposition out of which the remaining formations proceed. There is here,
essentially regarded, not a single characteristic utterance of the activity taking
place in the vascular layer which must not reflect in itself remembrances of the
functions taking place in the animal and the vegetative layer.
% (the chiasmus is letterspaced in the Danish exactly as set here: „animalt“
%  and „vegetativt“ gesperrt in the first limb, „saadant“ (twice) and „fordi“
%  (twice) in the second, while the SECOND „animalt“ and the SECOND
%  „vegetativt“ are NOT.)

With much assurance the empirical physiologist declares himself against the
principle of life as against a mystical-metaphysical presupposition which only
encumbers science; but by this it escapes his attention that in rejecting the
principle of life he at the same time rejects the principle of organization, and
that by

% --- p. 348 ---
rejecting the principle of organization he must absolutely arrive at a conception
without principle both of life and of the organism. In this respect too Lewes with
his sensualistic acuteness is a telling example. ``Aristotle,'' he says, ``assumed
that the phenomena of life were governed by different principles of life (souls,
ψυχαί, he calls them), which were separated each for itself but all subordinated to
one highest principle. This representation ruled for many centuries and was called
back to life a few years ago by Prout. Kant defines life as ``an inner principle of
activity'': an organism is that in which every part is at once means and aim.'' One
can with the same right say this of fermentation. Treviranus calls life ``the
constant uniformity of phenomena under the difference of outer influences'', which
can just as well be said of a clock, since the phenomena cease to be uniform as
soon as outer influences in any way disturb the organism or the mechanism. Bichat's
famous definition, ``life is the sum of the functions by which death is resisted'',
is only another form of Stahl's definition and is in every respect untenable; for
on the one side it is only a paraphrase of the tautology that life is the means by
which we live, and on the other side it declares that life meets an unhappy
resistance in the outer potencies, while nothing is more certain than that outer
potencies are a \emph{necessary condition} for the phenomena of life .... Dugés
calls life ``the particular activity of organized beings'', and Béclard says: ``life
is the sum of the phenomena proper to organized beings. It consists essentially in
this, that all organized beings are for a certain time mid-points into which
foreign substances penetrate and are appropriated, and out of which others go
forth.'' De Blainville's definition, which has been taken up by Auguste Comte and
Charles Robin, runs thus: ``life is the double inner movement of composition and
dissolution,
% (as printed in the Danish: ψυχαί is set in Greek types against the Fraktur
%  and, per the precedent of (διότι)/(οὗ ἕνεκα) earlier in the book, takes no
%  `\textit`. The proper names Dugés, Béclard, De Blainville, Auguste Comte,
%  Charles Robin, Treviranus, Bichat, Stahl and Prout are all Fraktur.)

% --- p. 349 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
which takes place everywhere at once and uninterruptedly''; but this fits only
nutrition and does not comprise all the main phenomena, while in the definition
proposed by Herbert Spencer --- namely, ``life is the definite combination of
heterogeneous changes, both simultaneous and successive, in correspondence with
external coexistences and sequences'' --- there is the drawback that it is not
perfectly intelligible when torn loose from his detailed explanation of it. ---
Perhaps the following definition would be most intelligible and easiest to
remember: ``\emph{life is the dynamical condition of the
organism}''\footnote{Hverdagsl. Physiol. 2. pp. 437--38.}.

To grasp what the physiologist here thinks of by an organism, we shall hear him
further draw a comparison between the organism and the machine. ``Without life the
organism is without movement. As little as we can conceive the clock set going by a
feeling, can we conceive the organism working without life. This is the cause of
the disinclination often felt to grasp the organism as a machine; but this
disinclination will yield when we consider how wonderfully the one member is joined
to the other in a whole consisting of many parts, and compare it with the somewhat
similar jointure of a machine, and at the same time remember that \emph{likeness} is
not \emph{sameness}, and that the organism, although it is a machine, is a
\emph{living} machine whose life is a source of great unlikenesses .... An organism
is a machine and works mechanically, in so far as its movements are
\emph{necessarily determined by the jointure of its organs.} But the organism
differs from the machine in this, that it is living, that it has sensibility for its
mainspring; and its movements, which are called mechanical or automatic, \emph{are
all determined by an impulse proceeding from guiding sensations.} It differs

% --- p. 350 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
further from a machine in this, that new capacities can develop in it .... New
connections between the single members arise, so that a combination of movements
which today is impossible may perhaps be easy in a
month\footnote{Work cited 2. pp. 166--170.}.''

Without dwelling on the author's highly cursory and superficial conception of
Kant's definition, or on the remarks with which he dismisses the other
``attempts'', we shall merely draw attention to what is unsatisfactory in his own
approach to a closer fixing of the concept of life and of the organism. When it is
asked what an organism is, he refers to life; and when it is asked what life is, he
refers to the organism.

By placing the organism's essence exclusively in ``a sum of conditions'' one makes
the law of otherness life's fundamental law. When it is said that ``life is the
dynamical condition of the organism'', one would almost assume that this dynamical
condition, which is ``a source of so ``great unlikenesses'' between ``the machine''
and the organism, must in the author's opinion essentially be what one otherwise
calls the principle of life; but since this is expressly denied in another place
(Work cited 2. pp. 432--33), the proposed definition can only signify that life is a
condition for ``the whole sum of conditions necessary for the organism's activity''.
But what is such a condition conditioning all conditions, when one may not think
thereby of the expression, reflected in otherness's form, for selfhood, the
unconditioned? Only a turn of phrase!
% (PRINTER'S DEFECT in the Danish, reproduced: „en Kilde til saa „store
%  Uligheder“ carries TWO opening low quotes and one closing --- the second
%  „ stands at the head of a line, the older convention of repeating the
%  opening mark on a continuation line, used nowhere else in the book so far.
%  Quote balance for this span therefore runs +1 opener on both sides.)

Darkest of all, however, is the expression ``a living machine'', if one really is to
think something determinate by it and grasp it in a concept. That the organism is a
living machine can signify two altogether opposite things: it can signify that the
organism is living because it is a

% --- p. 351 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
machine, but it can also signify that the organism is living although it is a
machine. In the first case the organism's life rests on the perfection of the
machinery; in the last case the organic forms a characteristic opposition to the
mechanical. From whichever side we take it, the thing is equally absurd.

If we proceed from the first assumption, then it may indeed be conceded that our
machines --- threshing machines, carding machines, planing machines and so on ---
are, however ingeniously they may otherwise be contrived, still highly imperfect in
comparison with the mechanics that distinguish the more composite organism. But can
the organism's life really be regarded as a result of its more perfect machinery?
This way of seeing does not let itself be held fast. If life in the organism is an
effect of the perfection of a highly intricate machinery, how can one then regard
cell life as the original? how can the protozoa, and the lower animals generally, be
``living machines''? An organism can, as regards the machinery, show itself highly
imperfect and still be living; conversely a machine can distinguish itself by a high
degree of mechanical perfection and still be infinitely far from belonging among
living beings. The highest activity which a mechanical whole can develop by the
reciprocal action of its parts has its measure in ``living
force''\footnote{Living force (\textit{force vive}), a category introduced by
Leibniz, is denoted in rational mechanics by $mv^2$ or by $\frac{mv^2}{2}$, where
$m$ is the mass and $v$ the velocity. --- What has been adduced in the foregoing
--- in the treatment of ``the problem of infinity'' --- about the relation between
space $s$, time $t$, velocity $v$ and force $\varphi$ will, if we assume $\varphi$
constant so as to keep to a special example, be sufficient in this connection to
show how the expression $\frac{mv^2}{2}$ acquires its particular significance. From
$s = f(t)$ there follows $\frac{ds}{dt} = v$, $\frac{d^2s}{dt^2} = \varphi =
\frac{dv}{dt}$. In so far as $\varphi$, the accelerating force
(\textit{force accélératrice}), acts on the mass $m$, there arises the moving force
$m\varphi$ (\textit{force motrice}). If the moving force now acts for a given time,
it performs a work whose measure is the product of the moving force and the space
traversed in this time; thus $m\varphi ds$ is an element of work, and $m\varphi s$
a quantity of work. From $m\varphi = m\frac{dv}{dt}$ there follows $m\varphi dt =
mdv$. But $dt$ is, according to $\frac{ds}{dt} = v$, equal to $\frac{ds}{v}$; hence
$m\varphi ds = mvdv$. By integrating this equation one obtains $m\varphi s =
\frac{mv^2}{2}$, or $2m\varphi s = mv^2$ --- an expression which shows that
\emph{living force is the double quantity of work.}}; but living force is not yet
life.
% (this note runs over onto p. 352 in the Danish and is given WHOLE here.)

% --- p. 352 ---
A living machine! It is difficult to hold this concept fast; for what is a machine?
``A closed system of mechanical forces!'' --- By no means! A machine is not a whole
system; it is at most only a fragment of one. To grasp a mechanical whole as a
machine we must lay particular emphasis on the piecework, on the composition, on the
mediate. Just as the machine does not have its first mover in itself, neither does
it have its purpose in itself; its construction and use is in all parts determined
by outer purposiveness: it drudges under otherness's law, it is determined only to
work.

As machine the organism would thus have its determination outside itself, namely in
the work; as living machine it would have its purpose in itself, in the maintenance
of forces, in self-preservation. But so sharp is the opposition between the working
of forces and their maintenance that the one, in the strict mechanical sense,
directly cancels the other: in so far as a mechanical whole is machine and works,
% (the footnote block at the foot of p. 352 in the Danish is the continuation
%  of the p. 351 note, given whole at its anchor; no mark and no counter reset.)

% --- p. 353 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}
it expends the forces and cannot work toward any self-maintenance; in so far as it
is in its working laid out for maintaining the moving forces, it performs no work
and is not in the character of machine; \emph{thus: in so far as the organism is a
machine it cannot be living; in so far as it is living it cannot be machine.}
